From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia

Prime Minister of Pakistan from 2018 to 2022

This article is about the politician and former cricketer. For other people with the same name, see [Imran Khan (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imran_Khan_(disambiguation) "Imran Khan (disambiguation)").

**Imran Ahmed Khan Niazi**[&#8202;](#ref-back-5-0)[\[a\]](#refnote-5) (born 5 October 1952) is a Pakistani former [cricketer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cricketer "Cricketer"), [philanthropist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philanthropist "Philanthropist"), and politician who served as the 19th prime minister of Pakistan from August 2018 until April 2022. As a cricketer, he captained the Pakistan national cricket team to victory in the [1992 Cricket World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992_Cricket_World_Cup "1992 Cricket World Cup"). After retiring from cricket, he established the [Shaukat Khanum Memorial Cancer Hospital and Research Centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shaukat_Khanum_Memorial_Cancer_Hospital_and_Research_Centre "Shaukat Khanum Memorial Cancer Hospital and Research Centre"), Pakistan's first cancer hospital. He is the founder of the political party [Pakistan Tehreek-e-Insaf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_Tehreek-e-Insaf "Pakistan Tehreek-e-Insaf") (PTI) and was its chairman from 1996 to 2023.

Born in [Lahore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lahore "Lahore"), he graduated from [Keble College, Oxford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keble_College,_Oxford "Keble College, Oxford"). He began his [international cricket](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_cricket "International cricket") career in a [1971 Test series](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistani_cricket_team_in_England_in_1971 "Pakistani cricket team in England in 1971") against England. He advocated for neutral [umpiring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umpire_(cricket) "Umpire (cricket)") during his captaincy. He played his last Test in January 1992 against Sri Lanka and retired after the [1992 Cricket World Cup final](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992_Cricket_World_Cup_final "1992 Cricket World Cup final"). In 2009, he was inducted into the [ICC Cricket Hall of Fame](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ICC_Cricket_Hall_of_Fame "ICC Cricket Hall of Fame"). In his bachelorhood, Khan had several relationships. He married [Jemima Goldsmith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jemima_Goldsmith "Jemima Goldsmith") in 1995, with whom he had two sons; [Reham Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reham_Khan "Reham Khan") in 2015; and [Bushra Bibi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bushra_Bibi "Bushra Bibi") in 2018. His first two marriages ended in divorce.

He supported [General Musharraf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Musharraf "General Musharraf")'s [1999 Pakistani coup d'état](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1999_Pakistani_coup_d'%C3%A9tat "1999 Pakistani coup d'état") but fell out with him around July 2002, and was first elected to the National Assembly of Pakistan in the 2002 Pakistani general election. In the [2018 Pakistani general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Pakistani_general_election "2018 Pakistani general election"), he contested five constituencies and became the first person in Pakistan's electoral history to win all five; he subsequently became prime minister in a PTI-led coalition government. During his [premiership](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Premiership_of_Imran_Khan "Premiership of Imran Khan"), he launched Pakistan's largest [welfare programme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ehsaas_Programme "Ehsaas Programme") during the COVID-19 pandemic and spoke out against [Islamophobia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamophobia "Islamophobia") in the [Western world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_world "Western world"). In February 2022, he became the first Pakistani prime minister since 2002 to visit Moscow, arriving on the day the [Russian invasion of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine "Russian invasion of Ukraine") began. In April 2022, he became the first Pakistani prime minister to be removed from office through a [no-confidence motion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/No-confidence_motion_against_Imran_Khan "No-confidence motion against Imran Khan"). He alleged [US involvement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cypher_No._I-0678 "Cypher No. I-0678") in his removal, blaming Washington for opposing his foreign policy that sought closer relations with China and Russia.

In October 2022, the Election Commission barred him from holding elected office for one term over the [Toshakhana case](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toshakhana_case "Toshakhana case"). The following month, he survived an [assassination attempt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attempted_assassination_of_Imran_Khan "Attempted assassination of Imran Khan"). In May 2023, he was arrested in the [Al-Qadir Trust case](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Qadir_Trust_case "Al-Qadir Trust case"), triggering the [May 9 riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/May_9_riots "May 9 riots"); although the Supreme Court later declared his arrest unlawful due to violation of judicial sanctity, he was arrested again in August. In January 2025, he was sentenced to 14 years' imprisonment in the Al-Qadir Trust case, followed in December by a further 17-year sentence and a fine in the [Toshakhana-II case](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toshakhana_reference_cases_against_Imran_Khan_and_Bushra_Bibi#Second_NAB_toshakhana_reference_case "Toshakhana reference cases against Imran Khan and Bushra Bibi"). He has alleged that [his imprisonment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imprisonment_of_Imran_Khan "Imprisonment of Imran Khan") is politically motivated, a claim denied by the [Shehbaz Sharif](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shehbaz_Sharif "Shehbaz Sharif") government and the post-2022 military establishment. He has been held in [Adiala Jail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adiala_Jail "Adiala Jail") in Rawalpindi since his arrest in 2023.

## Early life and family

Further information: [Family of Imran Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Family_of_Imran_Khan "Family of Imran Khan")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/43/JavedZamanWithImranKhan.jpg/250px-JavedZamanWithImranKhan.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:JavedZamanWithImranKhan.jpg) Khan with his uncle [Javed Zaman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Javed_Zaman_(Pakistani_cricketer) "Javed Zaman (Pakistani cricketer)"), who would later become his cricket mentor,[&#8202;](#ref-back-6-0)[\[5\]](#refnote-6)c.1957.

Khan was born in [Lahore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lahore "Lahore") on 5 October 1952.[&#8202;](#ref-back-7-0)[\[6\]](#refnote-7)[&#8202;](#ref-back-10-0)[\[b\]](#refnote-10) He is the only son of Ikramullah Khan Niazi, a civil engineer, and his wife, Shaukat Khanum, and has four sisters.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Warrior_Race-0)[\[9\]](#refnote-Warrior_Race) Paternally, he is of [Pathan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pathans_of_Punjab "Pathans of Punjab") origin from the [Niazi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Niazi "Niazi") tribe.[&#8202;](#ref-back-12-0)[\[10\]](#refnote-12)[&#8202;](#ref-back-13-0)[\[11\]](#refnote-13) His maternal family is from the [Burki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burki "Burki") community,[&#8202;](#ref-back-14-0)[\[12\]](#refnote-14) having lived near [Jalandhar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jalandhar "Jalandhar") for centuries before migrating to Pakistan after the [partition of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partition_of_India "Partition of India") in 1947.[&#8202;](#ref-back-15-0)[\[13\]](#refnote-15)[&#8202;](#ref-back-16-0)[\[14\]](#refnote-16)

He attended [Aitchison College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aitchison_College "Aitchison College") and Cathedral School in Lahore.[&#8202;](#ref-back-flamboyant-0)[\[15\]](#refnote-flamboyant)[&#8202;](#ref-back-18-0)[\[16\]](#refnote-18) He moved to [Royal Grammar School Worcester](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Grammar_School_Worcester "Royal Grammar School Worcester") in England in September 1971, where he performed well in [cricket](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cricket "Cricket").[&#8202;](#ref-back-19-0)[\[17\]](#refnote-19)[&#8202;](#ref-back-TCM-0)[\[18\]](#refnote-TCM) In 1972, after being turned down by [Cambridge University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cambridge_University "Cambridge University"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-ESPN-0)[\[19\]](#refnote-ESPN) he was admitted to [Keble College, Oxford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keble_College,_Oxford "Keble College, Oxford"), where he studied [philosophy, politics and economics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philosophy,_politics_and_economics "Philosophy, politics and economics"), graduating in 1975.[&#8202;](#ref-back-oxford_interview-0)[\[20\]](#refnote-oxford_interview) [Paul Hayes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Hayes_(historian) "Paul Hayes (historian)"), a cricket enthusiast, helped facilitate his entry to Oxford.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ESPN-1)[\[19\]](#refnote-ESPN)

## Personal life

Khan had several relationships during his bachelorhood.[&#8202;](#ref-back-23-0)[\[21\]](#refnote-23) His first girlfriend, [Emma Sergeant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emma_Sergeant "Emma Sergeant"), an artist and daughter of British investor Sir [Patrick Sergeant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patrick_Sergeant "Patrick Sergeant"), began a relationship with him in 1982.[&#8202;](#ref-back-mysteryblondes-0)[\[22\]](#refnote-mysteryblondes) They visited Pakistan together, including during a hunting trip to [Peshawar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peshawar "Peshawar") and a cricket tour to Australia.[&#8202;](#ref-back-maidens-0)[\[23\]](#refnote-maidens) Sergeant also painted his portrait during their relationship at his request.[&#8202;](#ref-back-26-0)[\[24\]](#refnote-26) Khan's [Chelsea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chelsea,_London "Chelsea, London") residence, near Sergeant's studio and [Tramp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tramp_(nightclub) "Tramp (nightclub)"), became a focus of his social life during this period. The relationship ended in 1986 after periods of separation.[&#8202;](#ref-back-mysteryblondes-1)[\[22\]](#refnote-mysteryblondes)

Ana-Luisa White, daughter of British industrialist [Gordon White](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gordon_White,_Baron_White_of_Hull "Gordon White, Baron White of Hull") and later known as Sita, began a relationship with Khan in 1986, shortly after his relationship with Sergeant ended. The relationship lasted two years. White later became pregnant after an encounter in 1991 and gave birth to a daughter, Tyrian Jade,[&#8202;](#ref-back-Hutchins-Midgley-0)[\[25\]](#refnote-Hutchins-Midgley)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Morgan-0)[\[26\]](#refnote-Morgan) in June 1992. She subsequently claimed that Khan was the father.[&#8202;](#ref-back-maidens-1)[\[23\]](#refnote-maidens) In 1997, a [California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/California "California") court ruled that Khan was Tyrian's father after White filed a paternity suit. The ruling followed his failure to respond to the suit or a request for a blood test.[&#8202;](#ref-back-29-0)[\[27\]](#refnote-29) Khan denied paternity and said he would take a [paternity test](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paternity_test "Paternity test") in Pakistan and accept the decision of the Pakistani courts.[&#8202;](#ref-back-30-0)[\[28\]](#refnote-30) After White's death in 2004, [Jemima](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jemima_Goldsmith "Jemima Goldsmith"), Khan's wife and a friend of White, was named Tyrian's legal guardian in White's will. Khan said Tyrian could join their family in London, leaving the decision to Jemima because of her relationship with his and Jemima's sons.[&#8202;](#ref-back-31-0)[\[29\]](#refnote-31)

In August 1992, Khan began dating [Kristiane Backer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kristiane_Backer "Kristiane Backer"), a German MTV host. Their relationship lasted nearly three years, during which Khan introduced her to Islam.[&#8202;](#ref-back-32-0)[\[30\]](#refnote-32)

Khan and [Jemima Goldsmith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jemima_Goldsmith "Jemima Goldsmith") confirmed their engagement on 12 May 1995. They married in a brief Islamic [Nikah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikah "Nikah") ceremony in Paris on 16 May, followed by a [civil ceremony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_ceremony "Civil ceremony") at the [Richmond](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richmond,_London "Richmond, London") register office on 21 June before moving to Lahore. Khan said Jemima converted to Islam "through her own convictions" after studying the religion. They have two sons, Sulaiman Isa and Kasim.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Independent1995-0)[\[31\]](#refnote-Independent1995)[&#8202;](#ref-back-34-0)[\[32\]](#refnote-34) On 22 June 2004, the couple divorced after nine years of marriage, with the divorce attributed to difficulties Jemima experienced adapting to life in Pakistan.[&#8202;](#ref-back-35-0)[\[33\]](#refnote-35)

On 8 January 2015, Khan married British-Pakistani journalist [Reham Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reham_Khan "Reham Khan") in a private Nikah ceremony at his Islamabad residence.[&#8202;](#ref-back-36-0)[\[34\]](#refnote-36) They announced their divorce on 22 October 2015.[&#8202;](#ref-back-37-0)[\[35\]](#refnote-37)

In 2016, media reports speculated about a possible third marriage involving Khan and his spiritual guide [Bushra Bibi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bushra_Bibi "Bushra Bibi"). Khan dismissed the reports as "absolutely baseless",[&#8202;](#ref-back-38-0)[\[36\]](#refnote-38) while members of the Manika family also denied the rumour.[&#8202;](#ref-back-39-0)[\[37\]](#refnote-39) Khan criticised the media for spreading the reports, and PTI filed a complaint against the channels involved.[&#8202;](#ref-back-40-0)[\[38\]](#refnote-40)[&#8202;](#ref-back-41-0)[\[39\]](#refnote-41)

In 2017, PTI member [Ayesha Gulalai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayesha_Gulalai "Ayesha Gulalai") alleged that Khan, then the party chairman, had sexually harassed her.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEWaseem2022-0)[\[40\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEWaseem2022) Gulalai said he had sent her "inappropriate text messages",[&#8202;](#ref-back-43-0)[\[41\]](#refnote-43) with the first allegedly sent in October 2013.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ids-0)[\[42\]](#refnote-ids)[&#8202;](#ref-back-45-0)[\[43\]](#refnote-45) Khan said he had not sent "indecent messages" to Gulalai and supported Prime Minister [Shahid Khaqan Abbasi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shahid_Khaqan_Abbasi "Shahid Khaqan Abbasi")'s proposal for a special committee to investigate the allegations. He later opposed the proposed committee's composition, calling for an independent panel to assess the allegations first.[&#8202;](#ref-back-probe-0)[\[44\]](#refnote-probe)[&#8202;](#ref-back-dawn080817-0)[\[45\]](#refnote-dawn080817) Khan and his party filed a defamation suit against Gulalai.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ids-1)[\[42\]](#refnote-ids)[&#8202;](#ref-back-48-0)[\[46\]](#refnote-48)[&#8202;](#ref-back-49-0)[\[47\]](#refnote-49) No inquiry was ultimately conducted and Gulalai did not publicly release the alleged messages.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ids-2)[\[42\]](#refnote-ids)

On 7 January 2018, the PTI central secretariat said Khan had proposed marriage to Bushra, but that she had not yet accepted.[&#8202;](#ref-back-50-0)[\[48\]](#refnote-50) On 18 February, PTI confirmed that Khan had married [Bushra Bibi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bushra_Bibi "Bushra Bibi").[&#8202;](#ref-back-51-0)[\[49\]](#refnote-51) The [mufti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mufti "Mufti") who conducted the marriage later testified that the nikah had been performed twice. He said the first ceremony, on 1 January 2018, took place while Bushra was still in _[iddat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iddat "Iddat")_, and alleged that he had believed the marriage would help Khan become prime minister.[&#8202;](#ref-back-52-0)[\[50\]](#refnote-52)

## Cricket career

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e8/Imran_Khan_at_Sydney_University_Luncheon%2C_1984.png/250px-Imran_Khan_at_Sydney_University_Luncheon%2C_1984.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Imran_Khan_at_Sydney_University_Luncheon,_1984.png) Khan as a test cricketer at a luncheon with [Sydney University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sydney_University "Sydney University")'s Vice-Chancellor [John Manning Ward](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Manning_Ward "John Manning Ward") before playing for the university club. Seated: Imran Khan, Chancellor Sir [Hermann Black](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hermann_Black "Hermann Black"), Coach John Dyson. Ward stands second from left, 1984

Khan made his [first-class](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First-class_cricket "First-class cricket") debut at 16 in [Lahore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lahore "Lahore"). In the early 1970s, he played for several Lahore-based teams, including Lahore A, Lahore B, Lahore Greens and [Lahore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lahore_cricket_teams "Lahore cricket teams").[&#8202;](#ref-back-53-0)[\[c\]](#refnote-53)[&#8202;](#ref-back-cricket_archive-0)[\[51\]](#refnote-cricket_archive) He played for the [Oxford University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oxford_University "Oxford University") cricket team from 1973 to 1975, earning a [Blue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_(university_sport) "Blue (university sport)").[&#8202;](#ref-back-oxford_interview-1)[\[20\]](#refnote-oxford_interview) Between 1971 and 1976, Khan played English county cricket for [Worcestershire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Worcestershire_County_Cricket_Club "Worcestershire County Cricket Club"), and also represented Dawood Industries[&#8202;](#ref-back-55-0)[\[d\]](#refnote-55) and [Pakistan International Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_International_Airlines_cricket_team "Pakistan International Airlines cricket team").[&#8202;](#ref-back-56-0)[\[e\]](#refnote-56) From 1983 to 1988, he played for [Sussex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sussex_County_Cricket_Club "Sussex County Cricket Club").[&#8202;](#ref-back-cricket_archive-1)[\[51\]](#refnote-cricket_archive)

Khan made his [Test cricket](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Test_cricket "Test cricket") debut against England in June 1971 at [Edgbaston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edgbaston_cricket_ground "Edgbaston cricket ground").[&#8202;](#ref-back-57-0)[\[52\]](#refnote-57) In August 1974, he played his first [One Day International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One_Day_International "One Day International") (ODI), against England at [Trent Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trent_Bridge "Trent Bridge") in the Prudential Trophy.[&#8202;](#ref-back-58-0)[\[53\]](#refnote-58)

He received the [Wetherell Award](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Cricket_Society "The Cricket Society") for leading all-rounder in English first-class cricket in 1976 and 1980.[&#8202;](#ref-back-59-0)[\[54\]](#refnote-59) By 1977, he was playing in [Kerry Packer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kerry_Packer "Kerry Packer")'s [World Series Cricket](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Series_Cricket "World Series Cricket").[&#8202;](#ref-back-60-0)[\[55\]](#refnote-60)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b0/Imran_Khans_bowling_statistic.png/250px-Imran_Khans_bowling_statistic.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Imran_Khans_bowling_statistic.png) Khan's [bowling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bowling_(cricket) "Bowling (cricket)") statistics as a cricketer from 1971 to 1991.

As a [bowler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bowler_(cricket) "Bowler (cricket)"), Khan learned [reverse swing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reverse_swing "Reverse swing") from [Sarfraz Nawaz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarfraz_Nawaz "Sarfraz Nawaz") and later taught it to [Wasim Akram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wasim_Akram "Wasim Akram") and [Waqar Younis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waqar_Younis "Waqar Younis").[&#8202;](#ref-back-61-0)[\[56\]](#refnote-61)[&#8202;](#ref-back-62-0)[\[57\]](#refnote-62) From January 1980 to 1988, he took 236 Test [wickets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dismissal_(cricket) "Dismissal (cricket)") at an [average](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bowling_average "Bowling average") of 17.77, including 18 [five-wicket hauls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Five-wicket_haul "Five-wicket haul") and five [10-wicket hauls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/10-wicket_haul "10-wicket haul"). His figures compared favourably with those of [Richard Hadlee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Hadlee "Richard Hadlee"), [Malcolm Marshall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malcolm_Marshall "Malcolm Marshall"), [Dennis Lillee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dennis_Lillee "Dennis Lillee"), [Joel Garner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joel_Garner "Joel Garner") and [Michael Holding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Holding "Michael Holding") in both average and [strike rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strike_rate "Strike rate").[&#8202;](#ref-back-63-0)[\[58\]](#refnote-63) He was the leading Test wicket-taker in 1982.[&#8202;](#ref-back-64-0)[\[59\]](#refnote-64) He recorded his best Test figures, taking 8 wickets for 58 [runs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Run_(cricket) "Run (cricket)") against [Sri Lanka in 1981–82](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sri_Lankan_cricket_team_in_Pakistan_in_1981%E2%80%9382 "Sri Lankan cricket team in Pakistan in 1981–82").[&#8202;](#ref-back-stats2-0)[\[60\]](#refnote-stats2)

### Captaincy

In 1982, Khan became captain of the Pakistan national cricket team.[&#8202;](#ref-back-stats1-0)[\[61\]](#refnote-stats1) Under his captaincy, Pakistan won their first Test in England in 28 years at [Lord's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lord's "Lord's") in 1982.[&#8202;](#ref-back-English_Test_win-0)[\[62\]](#refnote-English_Test_win) In 1983, he was named a [Wisden Cricketer of the Year](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisden_Cricketer_of_the_Year "Wisden Cricketer of the Year").[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEWilde2013-0)[\[63\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEWilde2013) During [Pakistan's 1982–83 series against India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_cricket_team_in_Pakistan_in_1982%E2%80%9383 "Indian cricket team in Pakistan in 1982–83"), he took 40 wickets at an average of 13.95.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEOborne2015299-0)[\[64\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEOborne2015299) In January 1983, he became the second cricketer, after [Ian Botham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ian_Botham "Ian Botham"), to score a [century](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Century_(cricket) "Century (cricket)") and take 10 wickets in a Test.[&#8202;](#ref-back-70-0)[\[65\]](#refnote-70) The following month, he reached a Test bowling rating of 922,[&#8202;](#ref-back-71-0)[\[f\]](#refnote-71) the highest [International Cricket Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Cricket_Council "International Cricket Council") (ICC) rating since World War I,[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEBermange2020-0)[\[66\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEBermange2020) and third-highest in all-time ICC Test bowling.[&#8202;](#ref-back-73-0)[\[67\]](#refnote-73) He became the first Pakistani bowler to take 200 Test wickets.[&#8202;](#ref-back-74-0)[\[68\]](#refnote-74) The series caused a stress fracture in his left shin, sidelining him for more than two years. Initially, he was able to bat but not bowl, before the injury eventually prevented him from playing altogether. An experimental treatment in London, funded by the Pakistani government, helped him recover by late 1984. He returned under [Javed Miandad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Javed_Miandad "Javed Miandad")'s captaincy in the 1984–85 home series against Sri Lanka. Miandad subsequently stepped down as captain, citing limited co-operation from Khan, who was then reappointed captain.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Oborne-0)[\[69\]](#refnote-Oborne)

Khan initiated reforms in cricket [umpiring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umpire_(cricket) "Umpire (cricket)") to reduce criticism of Pakistani officials in home series.[&#8202;](#ref-back-76-0)[\[70\]](#refnote-76) In 1986, he invited Indian umpires [VK Ramaswamy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/V._K._Ramaswamy_(umpire) "V. K. Ramaswamy (umpire)") and [Piloo Reporter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Piloo_Reporter "Piloo Reporter") to officiate a [Test against West Indies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Indian_cricket_team_in_Pakistan_in_1986%E2%80%9387 "West Indian cricket team in Pakistan in 1986–87"). He subsequently brought England's [John Hampshire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Hampshire "John Hampshire") and [John Holder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Holder_(umpire) "John Holder (umpire)") to officiate in the [home series against India in 1989–90](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_cricket_team_in_Pakistan_in_1989%E2%80%9390 "Indian cricket team in Pakistan in 1989–90"). The initiatives contributed to the introduction of a rule in 1994 requiring one neutral umpire and a 2002 rule requiring both umpires to be independent.[&#8202;](#ref-back-77-0)[\[71\]](#refnote-77)

In 1987, Khan led Pakistan to its first Test series win in India, followed by its first series victory in [England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistani_cricket_team_in_England_in_1987 "Pakistani cricket team in England in 1987") the same year.[&#8202;](#ref-back-English_Test_win-1)[\[62\]](#refnote-English_Test_win) He retired from international cricket after the [1987 Cricket World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1987_Cricket_World_Cup "1987 Cricket World Cup"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEOborne2015322-0)[\[72\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEOborne2015322) but returned at the request of [General Zia-ul-Haq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Zia-ul-Haq "General Zia-ul-Haq") and resumed the captaincy.[&#8202;](#ref-back-79-0)[\[73\]](#refnote-79) In 1988, he led Pakistan on a successful West Indies tour,[&#8202;](#ref-back-2006_profile-0)[\[74\]](#refnote-2006_profile) where he was named [Man of the Series](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Man_of_the_Series "Man of the Series") after taking 23 wickets in three Tests.[&#8202;](#ref-back-81-0)[\[75\]](#refnote-81)[&#8202;](#ref-back-82-0)[\[76\]](#refnote-82) He became the first Asian and eighth non-Australian to win the [International Cricketer of the Year](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Cricketer_of_the_Year "International Cricketer of the Year") in 1989.[&#8202;](#ref-back-83-0)[\[77\]](#refnote-83)

As captain, he led Pakistan in 48 Tests (14 wins, 8 losses, 26 draws) and 139 ODIs (75 wins, 59 losses, 1 tie).[&#8202;](#ref-back-stats1-1)[\[61\]](#refnote-stats1) In Tests, he holds the record for most wickets as captain, second-best [innings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Innings "Innings") figures (8/60), most five-wicket hauls in an innings (12), and most ten-wicket match hauls (4).[&#8202;](#ref-back-84-0)[\[78\]](#refnote-84)

#### 1992 Cricket World Cup final

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c7/Imran_Khan_at_the_1992_Cricket_World_Cup_Ceremony.jpg/250px-Imran_Khan_at_the_1992_Cricket_World_Cup_Ceremony.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Imran_Khan_at_the_1992_Cricket_World_Cup_Ceremony.jpg) Khan presenting the World Cup to the then Prime Minister of Pakistan, [Nawaz Sharif](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nawaz_Sharif "Nawaz Sharif"), during a dinner held in the team's honour after their win in the 1992 World Cup.[&#8202;](#ref-back-85-0)[\[79\]](#refnote-85)

Khan led Pakistan to victory in the [1992 Cricket World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992_Cricket_World_Cup "1992 Cricket World Cup").[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEBooth2019-0)[\[80\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEBooth2019) In the final, he top-scored with 72 runs and took the match-winning final wicket himself.[&#8202;](#ref-back-sc1-0)[\[81\]](#refnote-sc1) This was Pakistan's first [World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cricket_World_Cup "Cricket World Cup") victory.[&#8202;](#ref-back-WC-0)[\[82\]](#refnote-WC)

> It was rock-bottom when we lost to South Africa. I told the boys they had to play as if they were cornered tigers. I told them to forget about bowling no-balls and wides, and just go out there and fight.[&#8202;](#ref-back-89-0)[\[83\]](#refnote-89)

### Retirement

He played his last Test in January 1992 against Sri Lanka and retired from international cricket after leading Pakistan to victory in the [1992 Cricket World Cup final](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992_Cricket_World_Cup_final "1992 Cricket World Cup final").[&#8202;](#ref-back-stats2-1)[\[60\]](#refnote-stats2)

### Career stats

See also: [List of international cricket five-wicket hauls by Imran Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_international_cricket_five-wicket_hauls_by_Imran_Khan "List of international cricket five-wicket hauls by Imran Khan")

He ended his career with 88 Tests, 126 innings, scoring 3,807 runs at an average of 37.69, including six centuries, 18 fifties, and a highest score of 136.[&#8202;](#ref-back-stats2-2)[\[60\]](#refnote-stats2) He had the second-highest all-time Test [batting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Batting_(cricket) "Batting (cricket)") average of 61.86 playing at number six in the [batting order](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Batting_order_(cricket) "Batting order (cricket)").[&#8202;](#ref-back-90-0)[\[84\]](#refnote-90) As a bowler, he took 362 Test wickets.[&#8202;](#ref-back-stats2-3)[\[60\]](#refnote-stats2) He has won the most [Player of the Series awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Player_of_the_Series_awards_(cricket) "Player of the Series awards (cricket)") for Pakistan in Test cricket, ranking fourth overall in Test history.[&#8202;](#ref-back-91-0)[\[85\]](#refnote-91) In ODIs, he played 175 matches, scoring 3,709 runs at an average of 33.41, with a highest score of 102 [not out](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Not_out "Not out").[&#8202;](#ref-back-stats2-4)[\[60\]](#refnote-stats2) His best ODI [bowling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bowling_(cricket) "Bowling (cricket)") was 6 for 14, the highest ODI innings figures recorded by any bowler in a defeat.[&#8202;](#ref-back-92-0)[\[86\]](#refnote-92) He achieved the [all-rounder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All-rounder "All-rounder")'s triple—3,000 runs and 300 wickets—in 75 Tests,[&#8202;](#ref-back-3rd_fastest-0)[\[87\]](#refnote-3rd_fastest) becoming one of the first four players to reach the mark. As of 2025, he is one of eleven players to have done so,[&#8202;](#ref-back-94-0)[\[88\]](#refnote-94) and his 75-Test achievement is the third-fastest ever.[&#8202;](#ref-back-3rd_fastest-1)[\[87\]](#refnote-3rd_fastest)

## Post-retirement from cricket

After retiring, Khan said he had engaged in [ball tampering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ball_tampering "Ball tampering") during [county cricket](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/County_cricket "County cricket"), saying he "occasionally scratched the side of the ball and lifted the [seam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quarter_seam "Quarter seam")," and defended it by saying it was a commonplace practice.[&#8202;](#ref-back-95-0)[\[89\]](#refnote-95) He announced his return as a domestic league coach in May 2003.[&#8202;](#ref-back-96-0)[\[90\]](#refnote-96)

He occasionally appeared as a cricket commentator on Star TV.[&#8202;](#ref-back-WP_profile-0)[\[91\]](#refnote-WP_profile) In 2004, during the Indian cricket team's tour of Pakistan, he appeared as a commentator on [TEN Sports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TEN_Sports "TEN Sports")' live show, _Straight Drive_.[&#8202;](#ref-back-98-0)[\[92\]](#refnote-98)

On 23 November 2005, Khan was appointed [chancellor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chancellor_(education) "Chancellor (education)") of the [University of Bradford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Bradford "University of Bradford").[&#8202;](#ref-back-99-0)[\[93\]](#refnote-99) In 2009, he was inducted into the [ICC Cricket Hall of Fame](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ICC_Cricket_Hall_of_Fame "ICC Cricket Hall of Fame").[&#8202;](#ref-back-100-0)[\[94\]](#refnote-100) On 26 February 2014, the [University of Bradford Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Bradford_Union "University of Bradford Union") moved a no-confidence motion to remove him for missing all graduation ceremonies since 2010.[&#8202;](#ref-back-101-0)[\[95\]](#refnote-101) On 30 November 2014, he stepped down, citing "increasing political commitments," although it was reported that he effectively resigned under pressure.[&#8202;](#ref-back-102-0)[\[96\]](#refnote-102)

### Philanthropy

Khan served as [UNICEF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UNICEF "UNICEF") Special Representative for Sports, promoting health and [immunisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immunisation "Immunisation") programmes in countries like Bangladesh, Pakistan, Sri Lanka, and Thailand.[&#8202;](#ref-back-103-0)[\[97\]](#refnote-103)[&#8202;](#ref-back-104-0)[\[98\]](#refnote-104) In 1994, he founded Pakistan's first cancer hospital, the [Shaukat Khanum Memorial Cancer Hospital and Research Centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shaukat_Khanum_Memorial_Cancer_Hospital_and_Research_Centre "Shaukat Khanum Memorial Cancer Hospital and Research Centre"), named after his mother,[&#8202;](#ref-back-times_profile-0)[\[99\]](#refnote-times_profile) who had died of cancer.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEKamran2024-0)[\[100\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEKamran2024) He raised funds by touring the country, with most donations coming from common people.[&#8202;](#ref-back-times_profile-1)[\[99\]](#refnote-times_profile) He aimed to provide free treatment to underprivileged patients, and as of 2024, 70% of patients receive free care.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEKamran2024-1)[\[100\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEKamran2024)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/38/Imran_Khan_at_Pakistan_Embassy_School%2C_Jeddah.jpg/250px-Imran_Khan_at_Pakistan_Embassy_School%2C_Jeddah.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Imran_Khan_at_Pakistan_Embassy_School,_Jeddah.jpg) Khan fundraising for [Shaukat Khanum Memorial Cancer Hospital and Research Centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shaukat_Khanum_Memorial_Cancer_Hospital_and_Research_Centre "Shaukat Khanum Memorial Cancer Hospital and Research Centre") in [Jeddah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeddah "Jeddah"), 1992

To aid victims of the [2001 Islamabad cloud burst](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2001_Islamabad_cloud_burst "2001 Islamabad cloud burst"), Khan auctioned his signed 1992 Cricket World Cup bat and match sweater; the bat alone fetched US$20,000. He said these were the last items he had, having earlier auctioned the rest for Shaukat Khanum Hospital.[&#8202;](#ref-back-107-0)[\[101\]](#refnote-107)

In December 2005, Khan, then Chancellor of the University of Bradford, signed a [memorandum of understanding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Memorandum_of_understanding "Memorandum of understanding") to establish [Namal College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Namal_College "Namal College") in [Mianwali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mianwali "Mianwali") as its associate college. Built by the Mianwali Development Trust on land donated by locals, it aimed to tackle unemployment through technical and vocational education.[&#8202;](#ref-back-108-0)[\[102\]](#refnote-108) The college opened in 2008.[&#8202;](#ref-back-109-0)[\[103\]](#refnote-109)

In 2006, while visiting his children in London, he also worked with the [Lord's Taverners](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lord's_Taverners "Lord's Taverners") cricket charity.[&#8202;](#ref-back-times_profile-2)[\[99\]](#refnote-times_profile)

Khan established The Imran Khan Foundation (IKF) in February 2006.[&#8202;](#ref-back-110-0)[\[104\]](#refnote-110) In January 2013, IKF launched a Rs30 million relief project for [internally displaced persons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internally_displaced_person "Internally displaced person") of the [Mehsud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mehsud "Mehsud") tribe from [North Waziristan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Waziristan "North Waziristan"), providing food, winter essentials, and tents to 2,600 families in [Tank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tank_(city) "Tank (city)"), [Khyber Pakhtunkhwa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khyber_Pakhtunkhwa "Khyber Pakhtunkhwa"), who had limited government assistance.[&#8202;](#ref-back-111-0)[\[105\]](#refnote-111)

## Political career prior to premiership

### Initial years

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/29/Imran_in_peshawar.jpg/250px-Imran_in_peshawar.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Imran_in_peshawar.jpg) Khan at a political rally in [Peshawar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peshawar "Peshawar") in 1996

In 1987, [President Zia-ul-Haq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_Zia-ul-Haq "President Zia-ul-Haq") offered Khan a post in the [Pakistan Muslim League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_Muslim_League "Pakistan Muslim League") (PML), which he declined. In 1993, he was appointed tourism ambassador in the caretaker government of [Moeenuddin Ahmad Qureshi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moeenuddin_Ahmad_Qureshi "Moeenuddin Ahmad Qureshi"), serving for three months until the government was dissolved. He entered politics in the mid-1990s in open alliance with former [Inter-Services Intelligence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inter-Services_Intelligence "Inter-Services Intelligence") (ISI) chief [Hamid Gul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamid_Gul "Hamid Gul").[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEClary2022-0)[\[106\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEClary2022) Khan joined Gul and [Muhammad Ali Durrani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_Ali_Durrani "Muhammad Ali Durrani") in planning to launch a "pressure group" to act as a civil society watchdog, but the group never materialised.[&#8202;](#ref-back-flamboyant-1)[\[15\]](#refnote-flamboyant)

In 1995, [Prime Minister Benazir Bhutto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_Minister_Benazir_Bhutto "Prime Minister Benazir Bhutto"), fearing a military coup that could make him prime minister, responded with punitive measures. State television refused to air archival cricket footage and banned fundraising advertisements for [Shaukat Khanum Memorial Cancer Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shaukat_Khanum_Memorial_Cancer_Hospital "Shaukat Khanum Memorial Cancer Hospital") during [Ramadan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramadan "Ramadan"). Authorities also spread rumours that he was under investigation for embezzlement and tax irregularities. Donations to the hospital halved that year as a result.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Tim-0)[\[107\]](#refnote-Tim)

On 25 April 1996, he founded [Pakistan Tehreek-e-Insaf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_Tehreek-e-Insaf "Pakistan Tehreek-e-Insaf") (PTI).[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTESadiq2017-0)[\[108\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTESadiq2017) [Nawaz Sharif](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nawaz_Sharif "Nawaz Sharif") invited him to join [his party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PML_(N) "PML (N)"), offering him the second-in-command position and 30 National Assembly seats; Khan declined.[&#8202;](#ref-back-115-0)[\[109\]](#refnote-115) In the [1997 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1997_Pakistani_general_election "1997 Pakistani general election"), he contested the National Assembly constituencies of NA-53 Mianwali and NA-94 Lahore as a PTI candidate but lost both to candidates of the [Pakistan Muslim League (N)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_Muslim_League_(N) "Pakistan Muslim League (N)") (PML (N)).[&#8202;](#ref-back-116-0)[\[110\]](#refnote-116)

Khan initially supported [General Musharraf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Musharraf "General Musharraf")'s [1999 Pakistani coup d'état](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1999_Pakistani_coup_d'%C3%A9tat "1999 Pakistani coup d'état"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-117-0)[\[111\]](#refnote-117) believing Musharraf would end corruption and the influence of political elites.[&#8202;](#ref-back-118-0)[\[112\]](#refnote-118) His PTI was among the parties that supported [Pervez Musharraf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pervez_Musharraf "Pervez Musharraf") in the [2002 Pakistani referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2002_Pakistani_referendum "2002 Pakistani referendum").[&#8202;](#ref-back-119-0)[\[113\]](#refnote-119) However, Khan's relationship with the regime deteriorated. In July 2002, Musharraf's principal secretary Tariq Aziz tried to persuade him to join a pro-military alliance, but the meeting ended in acrimony, with Aziz telling Khan that he would never win his seat.[&#8202;](#ref-back-120-0)[\[114\]](#refnote-120) Khan contested the [2002 Pakistani general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2002_Pakistani_general_election "2002 Pakistani general election"), saying that PTI would consider forming a coalition if it did not win a majority,[&#8202;](#ref-back-121-0)[\[115\]](#refnote-121) and was the party's only successful candidate.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEJaffrelot2015-0)[\[116\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEJaffrelot2015)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/9/93/Imran_Khan_-_portrait_%28cropped%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail_unscaled)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Imran_Khan_-_portrait_(cropped).jpg) Khan in 2007

In May 2005, [Hendrik Hertzberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hendrik_Hertzberg "Hendrik Hertzberg") described him as "most directly responsible" for drawing attention in the Muslim world to the alleged [desecration of the Qur'an](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2005_Quran_desecration_controversy "2005 Quran desecration controversy") at [Guantanamo Bay Naval Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guantanamo_Bay_Naval_Base "Guantanamo Bay Naval Base").[&#8202;](#ref-back-123-0)[\[117\]](#refnote-123) In August 2005, [Declan Walsh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Declan_Walsh_(journalist) "Declan Walsh (journalist)") called him a "miserable politician", saying "Khan's ideas and affiliations since entering politics in 1996 have swerved and skidded like a [rickshaw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auto_rickshaw "Auto rickshaw") in a rainshower... He preaches democracy one day but gives a vote to reactionary mullahs the next."[&#8202;](#ref-back-124-0)[\[118\]](#refnote-124) In March 2006, Khan was escorted home by police and placed under [house arrest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_arrest "House arrest") after threatening to protest against [President George W. Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_George_W._Bush "President George W. Bush") during his visit to Pakistan.[&#8202;](#ref-back-125-0)[\[119\]](#refnote-125)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a8/Iktearsoffpapers.jpg/250px-Iktearsoffpapers.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Iktearsoffpapers.jpg) Khan tearing his nomination paper for the National Assembly at a press conference; he boycotted the 2008 elections.

On 2 October 2007, as part of the [All Parties Democratic Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All_Parties_Democratic_Movement "All Parties Democratic Movement"), he joined 85 MPs in resigning from Parliament to protest the [2007 Pakistani presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007_Pakistani_presidential_election "2007 Pakistani presidential election"), which General Musharraf contested without resigning as army chief.[&#8202;](#ref-back-resignation-0)[\[120\]](#refnote-resignation) After Musharraf declared a [state of emergency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007_Pakistani_state_of_emergency "2007 Pakistani state of emergency") on 3 November 2007, Khan was placed under house arrest but later escaped.[&#8202;](#ref-back-127-0)[\[121\]](#refnote-127) He emerged from hiding on 14 November to join a student protest at the [University of the Punjab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_the_Punjab "University of the Punjab").[&#8202;](#ref-back-128-0)[\[122\]](#refnote-128) Activists from the [Islami Jamiat-e-Talaba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islami_Jamiat-e-Talaba "Islami Jamiat-e-Talaba"), the student wing of Jamaat-e-Islami, restrained him inside a building during the rally.[&#8202;](#ref-back-129-0)[\[123\]](#refnote-129) He was arrested during the protest and sent to the [Central Jail, Dera Ghazi Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Jail,_Dera_Ghazi_Khan "Central Jail, Dera Ghazi Khan"), where he spent a week before being released.[&#8202;](#ref-back-130-0)[\[124\]](#refnote-130)

He opposed the 2010 military operation in [Kala Dhaka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kala_Dhaka "Kala Dhaka").[&#8202;](#ref-back-131-0)[\[125\]](#refnote-131) On 30 October 2011, he addressed over 100,000 supporters at the [Minar-e-Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minar-e-Pakistan "Minar-e-Pakistan") in [Lahore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lahore "Lahore"), challenging government policies, and calling his movement "not a flood that is coming, but a tsunami."[&#8202;](#ref-back-132-0)[\[126\]](#refnote-132) Another gathering of at least 100,000 supporters occurred in [Karachi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi "Karachi") on 25 December 2011.[&#8202;](#ref-back-133-0)[\[127\]](#refnote-133)

In August 2012, the [Pakistani Taliban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistani_Taliban "Pakistani Taliban") issued death threats to him over his march to their tribal stronghold to protest [U.S. drone strikes in Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._drone_strikes_in_Pakistan "U.S. drone strikes in Pakistan"), calling him a "liberal" and "secular" — terms they equated with being an infidel.[&#8202;](#ref-back-134-0)[\[128\]](#refnote-134) On 1 October, they withdrew death threats and offered him protection for the rally. They "endorse\[&#8202;](#ref-back-135-0)[d\] Imran Khan's plea that drone strikes are against our sovereignty."[\[129\]](#refnote-135) On 6 October, he led a convoy of 10,000 to protest US drone strikes. Security personnel stopped the convoy just miles from [South Waziristan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Waziristan "South Waziristan")'s border after negotiations failed, and Khan announced that the rally would return to [Tank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tank_(city) "Tank (city)").[&#8202;](#ref-back-136-0)[\[130\]](#refnote-136)

### 2013 elections

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/15/PTI_Symbol_Elections.png/250px-PTI_Symbol_Elections.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:PTI_Symbol_Elections.png) Khan chose the [cricket bat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cricket_bat "Cricket bat") as the [electoral symbol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electoral_symbol "Electoral symbol") for his party in the 2013 elections, and it remained as such until 2023

On 21 April 2013, Khan launched his election campaign from Lahore, addressing thousands at [The Mall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Mall,_Lahore "The Mall, Lahore").[&#8202;](#ref-back-137-0)[\[131\]](#refnote-137) He campaigned on withdrawing Pakistan from the US-led [war on terror](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_on_terror "War on terror"), pursuing peace in the [Pashtun tribal belt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federally_Administered_Tribal_Areas "Federally Administered Tribal Areas"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-138-0)[\[132\]](#refnote-138) and introducing a uniform education system aimed at providing equal opportunities for children.[&#8202;](#ref-back-139-0)[\[133\]](#refnote-139) He concluded the campaign in South Punjab's [Seraiki belt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seraiki_belt "Seraiki belt") with rallies in several cities.[&#8202;](#ref-back-140-0)[\[134\]](#refnote-140)

On 7 May, four days before the election, Khan fell from a lifter while boarding a stage at a rally, sustaining head injuries,[&#8202;](#ref-back-141-0)[\[135\]](#refnote-141) and four fractured vertebrae.[&#8202;](#ref-back-VM-0)[\[136\]](#refnote-VM) He concluded the campaign by addressing a rally in Islamabad via [video link](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Video_link "Video link") from his hospital bed.[&#8202;](#ref-back-143-0)[\[137\]](#refnote-143)

The [2013 Pakistani general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_Pakistani_general_election "2013 Pakistani general election") was held on 11 May. The PML (N), headed by Nawaz Sharif, won, while Khan alleged vote-rigging.[&#8202;](#ref-back-144-0)[\[138\]](#refnote-144) The following day, [Asad Umar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asad_Umar "Asad Umar"), a leader from his party, announced that Khan had conceded defeat to PML (N).[&#8202;](#ref-back-145-0)[\[139\]](#refnote-145) While his party welcomed the vote, he pledged to release a [white paper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_paper "White paper") on alleged rigging and announced plans for protests.[&#8202;](#ref-back-146-0)[\[140\]](#refnote-146)

PTI became the leading party in the [Provincial Assembly of Khyber Pakhtunkhwa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Provincial_Assembly_of_Khyber_Pakhtunkhwa "Provincial Assembly of Khyber Pakhtunkhwa"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-147-0)[\[141\]](#refnote-147) and Khan nominated [Pervez Khattak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pervez_Khattak "Pervez Khattak") to lead its first provincial government.[&#8202;](#ref-back-148-0)[\[142\]](#refnote-148)

### In opposition

See also: [Inqilab March](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inqilab_March "Inqilab March")

Following the 2013 elections, Khan became PTI's parliamentary leader in the National Assembly.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Noria_Research-0)[\[143\]](#refnote-Noria_Research) On 31 July 2013, he received a contempt-of-court notice for allegedly describing the superior judiciary as "shameful."[&#8202;](#ref-back-150-0)[\[144\]](#refnote-150) The notice was discharged after he told the Supreme Court that his criticism had targeted lower-court judicial officers serving as returning officers during the elections.[&#8202;](#ref-back-151-0)[\[145\]](#refnote-151)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c4/Secretary_Kerry_Meets_With_Pakistani_Party_President_Imran_Khan.jpg/250px-Secretary_Kerry_Meets_With_Pakistani_Party_President_Imran_Khan.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Secretary_Kerry_Meets_With_Pakistani_Party_President_Imran_Khan.jpg) Khan meets U.S. Secretary of State [John Kerry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Kerry "John Kerry") in August 2013 while he was in opposition

Khan was criticised for supporting [Sami-ul-Haq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sami-ul-Haq "Sami-ul-Haq"), described as the "Father of the Taliban," and for funding his seminary, [Darul Uloom Haqqania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Darul_Uloom_Haqqania "Darul Uloom Haqqania").[&#8202;](#ref-back-152-0)[\[146\]](#refnote-152) He also accused the US of undermining peace with the Pakistani Taliban by killing its leader [Hakimullah Mehsud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hakimullah_Mehsud "Hakimullah Mehsud") in a 2013 drone strike. Khan announced plans to protest and block [NATO supply lines to Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NATO_logistics_in_the_Afghan_War "NATO logistics in the Afghan War") if drone attacks continued.[&#8202;](#ref-back-153-0)[\[147\]](#refnote-153)

In May 2014, Khan demanded the resignation of all [Election Commission of Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Election_Commission_of_Pakistan "Election Commission of Pakistan") (ECP) members and the formation of a new ECP, along with punishment for those he accused of stealing the electoral mandate.[&#8202;](#ref-back-154-0)[\[148\]](#refnote-154) In August, he led [a rally](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Tsunami_March "2014 Tsunami March") from Lahore to Islamabad, demanding Prime Minister Nawaz Sharif's resignation and an investigation into alleged electoral fraud.[&#8202;](#ref-back-155-0)[\[149\]](#refnote-155) He and Canadian-Pakistani cleric [Muhammad Tahir-ul-Qadri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_Tahir-ul-Qadri "Muhammad Tahir-ul-Qadri") formed a "de facto" alliance to mobilise supporters for a regime-change campaign against Sharif.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTENaseemullahChhibber2024-0)[\[150\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTENaseemullahChhibber2024)[&#8202;](#ref-back-157-0)[\[151\]](#refnote-157)

[Play video](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Pakistanprotests2014.webm "Play video")[Voice of America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voice_of_America "Voice of America") reports on Khan-led protest, August 2014

The 2014 protests also generated allegations of military involvement. Then-PTI president [Javed Hashmi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Javed_Hashmi "Javed Hashmi") alleged that Khan had been instructed by the army to coordinate the protests with [Pakistan Awami Tehreek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_Awami_Tehreek "Pakistan Awami Tehreek") (PAT),[&#8202;](#ref-back-Noria_Research-1)[\[143\]](#refnote-Noria_Research) and later accused him of conspiring with elements within the army.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Dawn2017-0)[\[152\]](#refnote-Dawn2017) [The New York Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_York_Times "The New York Times") described Hashmi's reference to a "signal" from outside as an apparent reference to military intervention.[&#8202;](#ref-back-159-0)[\[153\]](#refnote-159) In the _Routledge Handbook of Contemporary Pakistan_, Aparna Pande writes that parts of the army backed the protests, that Khan was advised by then ISI chief [Zaheer-ul-Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zaheer-ul-Islam "Zaheer-ul-Islam"), and that the ISI drafted Qadri to lead them.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEPande2017-0)[\[154\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEPande2017)

On 1 September, protesters led by Qadri and Khan attempted to storm Prime Minister Nawaz Sharif's official residence, triggering violence that left three people dead and more than 595 injured, including 115 police officers.[&#8202;](#ref-back-161-0)[\[155\]](#refnote-161) PTI and PAT protesters also stormed state-run [PTV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_Television "Pakistan Television"), assaulting staff.[&#8202;](#ref-back-162-0)[\[156\]](#refnote-162) Later that month, Khan urged supporters to burn electricity bills and join [civil disobedience](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_disobedience "Civil disobedience").[&#8202;](#ref-back-163-0)[\[157\]](#refnote-163) Following the [2014 Peshawar school massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Peshawar_school_massacre "2014 Peshawar school massacre"), he ended the 126-day sit-in on 17 December, citing the need for national unity.[&#8202;](#ref-back-164-0)[\[158\]](#refnote-164)

In March 2015, PTI and the [Sharif administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Nawaz_Sharif_government "Third Nawaz Sharif government") agreed to establish a judicial commission to investigate allegations of electoral fraud, with the move facilitated by Army Chief [General Raheel Sharif](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Raheel_Sharif "General Raheel Sharif").[&#8202;](#ref-back-165-0)[\[159\]](#refnote-165) In July, the commission found the elections broadly fair and found no systemic rigging.[&#8202;](#ref-back-166-0)[\[160\]](#refnote-166) Khan accepted its findings but said he had not received a copy of the report.[&#8202;](#ref-back-167-0)[\[161\]](#refnote-167)

Khan subsequently campaigned against Sharif following the [Panama Papers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panama_Papers "Panama Papers") leak in 2016, leading protests and calling for his resignation over corruption allegations.[&#8202;](#ref-back-168-0)[\[162\]](#refnote-168)

### 2018 elections campaign

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3b/Imran_Khan_in_Sindh%2C_2017.jpg/250px-Imran_Khan_in_Sindh%2C_2017.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Imran_Khan_in_Sindh,_2017.jpg) Khan campaigns for the elections in [Sindh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sindh "Sindh") while wearing a traditional [Sindhi cap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sindhi_cap "Sindhi cap") and [Ajrak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ajrak "Ajrak") in 2017

In the [2018 Pakistani general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Pakistani_general_election "2018 Pakistani general election"), Khan contested five National Assembly constituencies. He won all five seats, becoming the first politician in Pakistan to contest and win five constituencies in a single general election.[&#8202;](#ref-back-169-0)[\[163\]](#refnote-169)

Opposition parties alleged "massive rigging" and military influence in his favour.[&#8202;](#ref-back-170-0)[\[164\]](#refnote-170) [Nawaz Sharif](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nawaz_Sharif "Nawaz Sharif") and [PML (N)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PML_(N) "PML (N)") alleged that military alignment with the judiciary had affected the outcome in Khan's favour.[&#8202;](#ref-back-171-0)[\[165\]](#refnote-171) The Election Commission rejected the allegations.[&#8202;](#ref-back-172-0)[\[166\]](#refnote-172) Two days after the election, [Michael Gahler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Gahler "Michael Gahler"), chief observer of the European Union Election Observation Mission to Pakistan, said the overall situation was satisfactory.[&#8202;](#ref-back-173-0)[\[167\]](#refnote-173)

#### Nominations and appointments

On 6 August 2018, PTI nominated Khan for prime minister.[&#8202;](#ref-back-174-0)[\[168\]](#refnote-174) He designated [Asad Umar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asad_Umar "Asad Umar") as finance minister for his prospective government and nominated [Mahmood Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahmood_Khan "Mahmood Khan") for [Chief Minister of Khyber Pakhtunkhwa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chief_Minister_of_Khyber_Pakhtunkhwa "Chief Minister of Khyber Pakhtunkhwa") and [Usman Buzdar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Usman_Buzdar "Usman Buzdar") for [Chief Minister of Punjab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chief_Minister_of_Punjab_(Pakistan) "Chief Minister of Punjab (Pakistan)").[&#8202;](#ref-back-175-0)[\[169\]](#refnote-175)[&#8202;](#ref-back-176-0)[\[170\]](#refnote-176)[&#8202;](#ref-back-177-0)[\[171\]](#refnote-177) In [Balochistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balochistan,_Pakistan "Balochistan, Pakistan"), he directed PTI to support the [Balochistan Awami Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balochistan_Awami_Party "Balochistan Awami Party"), which nominated [Jam Kamal Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jam_Kamal_Khan "Jam Kamal Khan") as chief minister and [Abdul Quddus Bizenjo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdul_Quddus_Bizenjo "Abdul Quddus Bizenjo") as speaker.[&#8202;](#ref-back-178-0)[\[172\]](#refnote-178)

## Prime Minister (2018–2022)

Main article: [Premiership of Imran Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Premiership_of_Imran_Khan "Premiership of Imran Khan")

See also: [First 100 days of Imran Khan's premiership](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_100_days_of_Imran_Khan's_premiership "First 100 days of Imran Khan's premiership") and [Imran Khan government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imran_Khan_government "Imran Khan government")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a3/Imran_Khan_oath_as_Prime_Minister.jpg/250px-Imran_Khan_oath_as_Prime_Minister.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Imran_Khan_oath_as_Prime_Minister.jpg) Khan (left) is sworn in as Prime Minister, with President [Mamnoon Hussain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mamnoon_Hussain "Mamnoon Hussain") administering [the oath of office in 2018](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oath_of_office_ceremony_of_Imran_Khan "Oath of office ceremony of Imran Khan").

Khan was sworn in as Prime Minister of Pakistan on 18 August 2018 after his party, [Pakistan Tehreek-e-Insaf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_Tehreek-e-Insaf "Pakistan Tehreek-e-Insaf") (PTI), won the 2018 election.[&#8202;](#ref-back-179-0)[\[173\]](#refnote-179)

### Domestic initiatives

Khan emphasised austerity and reductions in government expenditure.[&#8202;](#ref-back-180-0)[\[174\]](#refnote-180) His government reduced the number of staff at the prime minister's residence from 524 to two and auctioned eight buffaloes and 61 luxury vehicles previously acquired for the Prime Minister's Office.[&#8202;](#ref-back-181-0)[\[175\]](#refnote-181)[&#8202;](#ref-back-182-0)[\[176\]](#refnote-182) He was nevertheless criticised for using a helicopter to commute.[&#8202;](#ref-back-183-0)[\[177\]](#refnote-183)

Khan launched the [Plant for Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plant_for_Pakistan "Plant for Pakistan") programme,[&#8202;](#ref-back-184-0)[\[178\]](#refnote-184) and announced the establishment of 15 [national parks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_national_parks_of_Pakistan "List of national parks of Pakistan") under the [Protected Areas Initiative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protected_areas_of_Pakistan "Protected areas of Pakistan").[&#8202;](#ref-back-185-0)[\[179\]](#refnote-185) During the Climate Ambition Summit in 2020 he said that Pakistan would scrap two coal projects (2,600 MW) and set a target of 60% renewable energy by 2030.[&#8202;](#ref-back-186-0)[\[180\]](#refnote-186) He attended the signing ceremony for the $2.5 billion [Kohala Hydropower Project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kohala_Hydropower_Project "Kohala Hydropower Project") (1,124 MW).[&#8202;](#ref-back-187-0)[\[181\]](#refnote-187)

He appointed Lt. General [Asim Munir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asim_Munir "Asim Munir") as [Director-General of Inter-Services Intelligence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Director-General_of_Inter-Services_Intelligence "Director-General of Inter-Services Intelligence") (DG-ISI),[&#8202;](#ref-back-188-0)[\[182\]](#refnote-188) _[The Daily Telegraph](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Daily_Telegraph "The Daily Telegraph")_ reported that Munir proposed an investigation into corruption allegations involving Khan's wife, [Bushra Bibi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bushra_Bibi "Bushra Bibi"). According to _The Daily Telegraph_, shortly afterward, Munir was removed as [Director-General of Inter-Services Intelligence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Director-General_of_Inter-Services_Intelligence "Director-General of Inter-Services Intelligence") (ISI). Khan said Munir's proposed investigation was not the reason for his removal.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Telegraph-0)[\[183\]](#refnote-Telegraph)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7f/Imran_Khan_%E2%80%93_Qanar_Javed_Bajwa.jpg/250px-Imran_Khan_%E2%80%93_Qanar_Javed_Bajwa.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Imran_Khan_%E2%80%93_Qanar_Javed_Bajwa.jpg) Imran Khan, accompanied by foreign minister [Shah Mehmood Qureshi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shah_Mehmood_Qureshi "Shah Mehmood Qureshi") and Chief of Army Staff [Qamar Javed Bajwa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qamar_Javed_Bajwa "Qamar Javed Bajwa")

Khan also appointed [Zaheer Ahmad Babar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zaheer_Ahmad_Babar "Zaheer Ahmad Babar") as Chief of Air Staff.[&#8202;](#ref-back-190-0)[\[184\]](#refnote-190) In 2019, Khan extended the tenure of General [Qamar Javed Bajwa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qamar_Javed_Bajwa "Qamar Javed Bajwa") as Chief of Army Staff.[&#8202;](#ref-back-191-0)[\[185\]](#refnote-191) At the time, he described Bajwa as a balanced personality who was fully committed to democracy, calling him the "best army chief".[&#8202;](#ref-back-192-0)[\[186\]](#refnote-192) In November 2022, Khan said he had offered Bajwa a second extension in March that year amid a [no-confidence move against his government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/No-confidence_motion_against_Imran_Khan "No-confidence motion against Imran Khan"). According to ISI chief Lt Gen [Nadeem Anjum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nadeem_Anjum "Nadeem Anjum"), Bajwa rejected that offer.[&#8202;](#ref-back-193-0)[\[187\]](#refnote-193) In December 2022, Khan said he had committed a "big mistake" by extending Bajwa's tenure in 2019.[&#8202;](#ref-back-194-0)[\[188\]](#refnote-194)

During the 2020 sugar price surge, Khan ordered an inquiry that implicated figures from both government and opposition parties and documented wrongdoing in the sugar industry.[&#8202;](#ref-back-195-0)[\[189\]](#refnote-195) In December 2020, Khan sought the extradition of former prime minister [Nawaz Sharif](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nawaz_Sharif "Nawaz Sharif") from London to face trial in the [Panama Papers case](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panama_Papers_case "Panama Papers case").[&#8202;](#ref-back-196-0)[\[190\]](#refnote-196) The 2022 Cabinet Division yearbook stated that the Assets Recovery Unit established by Khan helped government agencies recover Rs426.4 billion, although the unit itself did not directly recover assets.[&#8202;](#ref-back-197-0)[\[191\]](#refnote-197)

During [COVID-19 in Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_in_Pakistan "COVID-19 in Pakistan"), he ruled out a complete lockdown, citing extreme poverty.[&#8202;](#ref-back-198-0)[\[192\]](#refnote-198) Khan adopted a _smart lockdown_ strategy, which used military technology for tracking and tracing COVID-19 cases, targeting specific virus hotspots instead of implementing a nationwide lockdown.[&#8202;](#ref-back-199-0)[\[193\]](#refnote-199)[&#8202;](#ref-back-200-0)[\[194\]](#refnote-200) He said that strict lockdowns would devastate Pakistan's economy and lead to starvation.[&#8202;](#ref-back-npr-0)[\[195\]](#refnote-npr)

Khan elevated the status of the construction sector to that of an industry and provided incentives such as tax breaks, sales tax reduction in coordination with provinces, and a subsidy of Rs30 billion to the [Naya Pakistan Housing & Development Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naya_Pakistan_Housing_&_Development_Authority "Naya Pakistan Housing & Development Authority"). He said that investors in the industry would not be asked about their source of income for the year and removed withholding tax for all construction sectors except cement and steel. Additionally, capital gains tax was withdrawn for homeowners selling houses.[&#8202;](#ref-back-incentives-0)[\[196\]](#refnote-incentives) Khan launched Pakistan's largest welfare programme,[&#8202;](#ref-back-203-0)[\[197\]](#refnote-203) distributing a lump sum of Rs12,000 to 10 million low-income citizens under the [Ehsaas Programme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ehsaas_Programme "Ehsaas Programme").[&#8202;](#ref-back-204-0)[\[198\]](#refnote-204) Educationist [Michael Barber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Barber_(educationist) "Michael Barber (educationist)") said the Ehsaas Programme was Khan's flagship initiative and praised it for its transparency, multi-sectoral approach, and initiatives including _Ehsaas Kafaalat_ and _Nashonuma_.[&#8202;](#ref-back-205-0)[\[199\]](#refnote-205)

Khan called for debt relief for developing nations during the pandemic, a proposal that gained support from the United Nations and several African countries.[&#8202;](#ref-back-206-0)[\[200\]](#refnote-206)[&#8202;](#ref-back-207-0)[\[201\]](#refnote-207)

[Play video](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Prime_Minister_Imran_Khan_calls_for_global_debt_relief,_April_2020.webm "Play video") Duration: 2 minutes and 44 seconds.2:44Prime Minister Khan calls for global debt relief amidst the COVID-19 pandemic, April 2020

Pakistan faced a worsening [economic crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistani_economic_crisis_(2021%E2%80%932024) "Pakistani economic crisis (2021–2024)") towards the end of his term.[&#8202;](#ref-back-208-0)[\[202\]](#refnote-208) After his ouster, economist [Atif Mian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atif_Mian "Atif Mian") said that Khan had inherited a weak economy but left it in a worse condition, with no increase in average income and Pakistan remaining in a [balance of payments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balance_of_payments "Balance of payments") crisis.[&#8202;](#ref-back-209-0)[\[203\]](#refnote-209)

Khan initially held the [Ministry of Interior](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Interior_(Pakistan) "Ministry of Interior (Pakistan)") portfolio under himself after forming his cabinet.[&#8202;](#ref-back-210-0)[\[204\]](#refnote-210) In 2019, the Interior Ministry investigated social media users, including journalists, who posted images of murdered Saudi journalist [Jamal Khashoggi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jamal_Khashoggi "Jamal Khashoggi") during Saudi Crown Prince [Mohammed bin Salman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammed_bin_Salman "Mohammed bin Salman")'s visit to Pakistan.[&#8202;](#ref-back-211-0)[\[205\]](#refnote-211)[&#8202;](#ref-back-212-0)[\[206\]](#refnote-212) In 2021, the [International Press Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Press_Institute "International Press Institute") raised "grave concerns" in an open letter to Khan over the proposed Pakistan Media Development Authority ordinance.[&#8202;](#ref-back-213-0)[\[207\]](#refnote-213) A [Pakistan Press Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_Press_Foundation "Pakistan Press Foundation") report also criticised Khan's language towards journalists, saying that he referred to them as "mafias" and "blackmailers".[&#8202;](#ref-back-214-0)[\[208\]](#refnote-214)

In June 2021, Khan said that the rise in rape cases in Pakistan was linked to women wearing "very few clothes," claiming that such behaviour would "have an impact on the men unless they are robots." This comment was criticised by women's rights groups, accusing him of being a "rape apologist."[&#8202;](#ref-back-215-0)[\[209\]](#refnote-215) Khan later said the rapist is always solely responsible, the victim never is, and his earlier comments were taken out of context.[&#8202;](#ref-back-216-0)[\[210\]](#refnote-216)

### Foreign policy

See also: [List of international prime ministerial trips made by Imran Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_international_prime_ministerial_trips_made_by_Imran_Khan "List of international prime ministerial trips made by Imran Khan")

President Donald Trump tweeted in November 2018 that he would cut billions in aid to Pakistan, saying it had not done "a damn thing for us." Khan responded that U.S. aid was a "minuscule" $20 billion, while Pakistan had lost 75,000 lives and over $123 billion fighting the "US [war on terror](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_on_terror "War on terror")." Khan also said that Pakistan had provided supply routes to U.S. forces, asking, "Can Mr Trump name another ally that gave such sacrifices?"[&#8202;](#ref-back-217-0)[\[211\]](#refnote-217) He later told the US that Pakistan would no longer act as its "hired gun."[&#8202;](#ref-back-218-0)[\[212\]](#refnote-218)

After the [2019 Pulwama attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Pulwama_attack "2019 Pulwama attack") in [Indian-administered Kashmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jammu_and_Kashmir_(union_territory) "Jammu and Kashmir (union territory)"), India blamed Pakistan. Khan said Pakistan was not involved in the attack and authorised the military to respond with force to any Indian aggression.[&#8202;](#ref-back-219-0)[\[213\]](#refnote-219) After India's [2019 Balakot airstrike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Balakot_airstrike "2019 Balakot airstrike"), Pakistan carried out [Operation Swift Retort](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Swift_Retort "Operation Swift Retort"), during which Indian pilot [Abhinandan Varthaman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abhinandan_Varthaman "Abhinandan Varthaman") was captured. A few days later, Khan released him as a gesture of de-escalation.[&#8202;](#ref-back-220-0)[\[214\]](#refnote-220) Following the [Revocation of the special status of Jammu and Kashmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revocation_of_the_special_status_of_Jammu_and_Kashmir "Revocation of the special status of Jammu and Kashmir"), Khan suspended high-level engagement with India.[&#8202;](#ref-back-221-0)[\[215\]](#refnote-221) Khan inaugurated the [Kartarpur Corridor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kartarpur_Corridor "Kartarpur Corridor"), providing visa-free access to [Indian Sikh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Sikh "Indian Sikh") pilgrims.[&#8202;](#ref-back-222-0)[\[216\]](#refnote-222)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0b/Gurdwara_Darbar_Sahib_Kartarpur3.jpg/250px-Gurdwara_Darbar_Sahib_Kartarpur3.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Gurdwara_Darbar_Sahib_Kartarpur3.jpg) [Gurdwara Darbar Sahib](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gurdwara_Darbar_Sahib "Gurdwara Darbar Sahib") renovated by Khan's government

Khan supported Turkey during the [2019 Turkish offensive into north-eastern Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Turkish_offensive_into_north-eastern_Syria "2019 Turkish offensive into north-eastern Syria").[&#8202;](#ref-back-223-0)[\[217\]](#refnote-223) He supported the [Afghan peace process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_peace_process "Afghan peace process") and facilitated trade by inaugurating a [24/7 border crossing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torkham_border_crossing "Torkham border crossing") with Afghanistan.[&#8202;](#ref-back-224-0)[\[218\]](#refnote-224) In 2019, he was named in the _[Time 100](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_100 "Time 100")_ list of most influential people.[&#8202;](#ref-back-225-0)[\[219\]](#refnote-225) In June 2020, addressing the National Assembly of Pakistan, he called Osama bin Laden a _shaheed_ (martyr), drawing opposition criticism.[&#8202;](#ref-back-226-0)[\[220\]](#refnote-226)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0d/Vladimir_Putin_and_Imran_Khan_%282022-02-24%29_01.jpg/250px-Vladimir_Putin_and_Imran_Khan_%282022-02-24%29_01.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Vladimir_Putin_and_Imran_Khan_(2022-02-24)_01.jpg) Khan met with Russian President [Vladimir Putin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir_Putin "Vladimir Putin") in Moscow just hours after [Russia's invasion of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russia's_invasion_of_Ukraine "Russia's invasion of Ukraine") began.[&#8202;](#ref-back-227-0)[\[221\]](#refnote-227)

On 24 February 2022, Khan visited Moscow to discuss economic and energy cooperation with Russian president [Vladimir Putin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir_Putin "Vladimir Putin"), becoming the first Pakistani prime minister in two decades to visit Russia.[&#8202;](#ref-back-IND_Russia-0)[\[222\]](#refnote-IND_Russia) His visit took place amid heightened tensions over the prospect of a [Russian invasion of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine "Russian invasion of Ukraine"), which had seemed imminent before his arrival.[&#8202;](#ref-back-229-0)[\[223\]](#refnote-229) In a video recorded on his arrival, he was heard saying, "What a time I have come...so much excitement." A few hours after his arrival, Russia began its full scale invasion of Ukraine.[&#8202;](#ref-back-230-0)[\[224\]](#refnote-230) The visit drew criticism from the United States.[&#8202;](#ref-back-IND_Russia-1)[\[222\]](#refnote-IND_Russia)

#### FATF compliance

In June 2022, two months after his ouster, [FATF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FATF "FATF") removed Pakistan from the grey list, citing completion of the 2018 and 2021 action plans.[&#8202;](#ref-back-231-0)[\[225\]](#refnote-231) Khan attributed the progress to his government, particularly former energy minister [Hammad Azhar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hammad_Azhar "Hammad Azhar"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-232-0)[\[226\]](#refnote-232) while the succeeding [First Shehbaz Sharif government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Shehbaz_Sharif_government "First Shehbaz Sharif government") also claimed credit. _[The Express Tribune](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Express_Tribune "The Express Tribune")_ reported that Khan's government had "done most of the work."[&#8202;](#ref-back-NH-0)[\[227\]](#refnote-NH)

#### Stance regarding Islamophobia

Main article: [International Day to Combat Islamophobia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Day_to_Combat_Islamophobia "International Day to Combat Islamophobia")

[Play video](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:UN_should_declare_an_'International_Day_to_Combat_Islamophobia_-_Prime_Minister_Imran_Khan_at_UNGA.webm "Play video")While addressing the [Seventy-fifth session of the United Nations General Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seventy-fifth_session_of_the_United_Nations_General_Assembly "Seventy-fifth session of the United Nations General Assembly") in 2020, Prime Minister Khan suggested an international day to combat Islamophobia.[&#8202;](#ref-back-234-0)[\[228\]](#refnote-234)

Khan advocated international action against [Islamophobia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamophobia "Islamophobia").[&#8202;](#ref-back-235-0)[\[229\]](#refnote-235) In 2019, he joined Turkish president Recep Tayyip Erdoğan and Malaysian prime minister Mahathir Mohamad in announcing plans for an English-language television channel addressing Islamophobia.[&#8202;](#ref-back-236-0)[\[230\]](#refnote-236) In October 2020, Khan objected to the spread of Islamophobia, urging Facebook to ban related content and accusing French president Emmanuel Macron of attacking Islam for supporting cartoons of [Prophet Muhammad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prophet_Muhammad "Prophet Muhammad").[&#8202;](#ref-back-237-0)[\[231\]](#refnote-237) In 2021, Khan called on Muslim countries to press [the West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_world "Western world") to criminalise insults against Prophet Muhammad, likening it to laws against [Holocaust denial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holocaust_denial "Holocaust denial").[&#8202;](#ref-back-238-0)[\[232\]](#refnote-238) In March 2022, Pakistan led a UN resolution designating 15 March as the [International Day to Combat Islamophobia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Day_to_Combat_Islamophobia "International Day to Combat Islamophobia").[&#8202;](#ref-back-239-0)[\[233\]](#refnote-239)

### Removal from office

Further information: [2022 Pakistani constitutional crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Pakistani_constitutional_crisis "2022 Pakistani constitutional crisis") and [No-confidence motion against Imran Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/No-confidence_motion_against_Imran_Khan "No-confidence motion against Imran Khan")
