Opposition parties began planning a no-confidence motion against Khan by the end of 2021, with [Fazal-ur-Rehman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fazal-ur-Rehman_(politician,_born_1953) "Fazal-ur-Rehman (politician, born 1953)") making a formal announcement on 11 February 2022.[&#8202;](#ref-back-240-0)[\[234\]](#refnote-240) On 8 March, opposition parties filed a [no-confidence motion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/No-confidence_motion_against_Imran_Khan "No-confidence motion against Imran Khan"), alleging economic mismanagement, including rising inflation, high debt, and a weakening currency, as well as foreign-policy failures.[&#8202;](#ref-back-241-0)[\[235\]](#refnote-241) By 18 March, defections from his party and the departure of coalition partners had left his government without a majority in the National Assembly.[&#8202;](#ref-back-242-0)[\[236\]](#refnote-242)[&#8202;](#ref-back-aj180322-0)[\[237\]](#refnote-aj180322)

The crisis was accompanied by controversy over a [diplomatic cypher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cypher_No._I-0678 "Cypher No. I-0678") that Khan said showed foreign pressure for his removal. On 27 March, he displayed the cypher at a rally and alleged US involvement in the attempt to remove him.[&#8202;](#ref-back-244-0)[\[238\]](#refnote-244) The [National Security Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Security_Council_(Pakistan) "National Security Council (Pakistan)") subsequently expressed concern about foreign interference.[&#8202;](#ref-back-245-0)[\[239\]](#refnote-245)

On 3 April, President [Arif Alvi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arif_Alvi "Arif Alvi"), acting on Khan's advice, dissolved the National Assembly after the Deputy Speaker rejected the no-confidence motion. The opposition challenged the decision in the [Supreme Court of Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_Pakistan "Supreme Court of Pakistan"), which ruled the rejection unconstitutional and ordered the vote to proceed.[&#8202;](#ref-back-246-0)[\[240\]](#refnote-246) On 10 April, he sent the cypher to the Supreme Court, despite legal warnings about potential breaches of constitutional oaths and the [Official Secrets Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Official_Secrets_Act_(Pakistan) "Official Secrets Act (Pakistan)").[&#8202;](#ref-back-247-0)[\[241\]](#refnote-247) Later same day, the National Assembly voted to remove Khan, making him the first Pakistani prime minister to be removed through a no-confidence vote.[&#8202;](#ref-back-248-0)[\[242\]](#refnote-248)[&#8202;](#ref-back-249-0)[\[243\]](#refnote-249)

In August 2023, _[The Intercept](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Intercept "The Intercept")_ published the alleged cypher. According to the purported document, U.S. State Department officials, including [Donald Lu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Lu "Donald Lu"), raised concerns about Khan's stance on the Russian invasion of Ukraine on 8 March 2022. It reportedly said that Lu told Pakistan's ambassador that "all will be forgiven in Washington" if Khan was removed through a no-confidence motion, while indicating possible economic and political isolation if he remained. The U.S. denied trying to remove him, saying its concerns were about policy, not leadership.[&#8202;](#ref-back-voa-0)[\[244\]](#refnote-voa)

## Post-premiership

See also: [2022–2025 Pakistan political unrest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022%E2%80%932025_Pakistan_political_unrest "2022–2025 Pakistan political unrest")

After his removal, Khan again alleged US involvement, stating that the US opposed his foreign policy that strengthened ties with China and Russia.[&#8202;](#ref-back-251-0)[\[245\]](#refnote-251) In November 2022, when asked about the alleged conspiracy to remove him, he said it was "behind me" and said he wanted Pakistan to maintain good relations with all countries, particularly the United States.[&#8202;](#ref-back-252-0)[\[246\]](#refnote-252)

In a 2022 speech, Khan referenced a clip of [Maryam Nawaz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maryam_Nawaz "Maryam Nawaz") repeatedly mentioning him. He said: "She took my name so many times and with so much passion...Maryam, please be careful, your husband might get upset." According to _[Dawn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dawn_(newspaper) "Dawn (newspaper)")_, his remarks followed her repeated tirades against him and [Bushra Bibi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bushra_Bibi "Bushra Bibi").[&#8202;](#ref-back-253-0)[\[247\]](#refnote-253) The [Human Rights Commission of Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Rights_Commission_of_Pakistan "Human Rights Commission of Pakistan") condemned Khan's remarks, saying they "plumbed the depths of [misogyny](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Misogyny "Misogyny")" and demanded an apology to Maryam and all women.[&#8202;](#ref-back-254-0)[\[248\]](#refnote-254)

### Toshakhana reference and disqualification

The [First Shehbaz Sharif government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Shehbaz_Sharif_government "First Shehbaz Sharif government") filed the [Toshakhana case against Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toshakhana_reference_cases_against_Imran_Khan_and_Bushra_Bibi "Toshakhana reference cases against Imran Khan and Bushra Bibi") in August 2022, alleging he failed to disclose official gifts and their sale proceeds to the Election Commission of Pakistan (ECP),[&#8202;](#ref-back-255-0)[\[249\]](#refnote-255) and did not declare the proceeds for tax purposes.[&#8202;](#ref-back-256-0)[\[250\]](#refnote-256)

Khan reportedly retained 58 gifts, 14 valued over Rs 14,000 each. On 8 September, he rejected the allegations and asked the ECP to dismiss the case, saying all purchases were lawfully declared in his tax returns and wealth statements. Hearings were delayed as his legal team sought extensions.[&#8202;](#ref-back-257-0)[\[251\]](#refnote-257)

On 21 October 2022, the ECP disqualified Khan under [Article 63(1)(p) of the Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Article_62_and_63 "Article 62 and 63"), ruling he submitted a false statement and incorrect asset declaration for 2020–21. A four-member bench unanimously found he had misled officials about Toshakhana gifts and ordered criminal proceedings under the [Election Act, 2017](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Election_Act,_2017 "Election Act, 2017").[&#8202;](#ref-back-258-0)[\[252\]](#refnote-258)

### Assassination attempt

Main article: [Attempted assassination of Imran Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attempted_assassination_of_Imran_Khan "Attempted assassination of Imran Khan")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fa/Imran_Khan_injuries_2022.jpg/250px-Imran_Khan_injuries_2022.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Imran_Khan_injuries_2022.jpg) Khan speaking to media at [Shaukat Khanum hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shaukat_Khanum_hospital "Shaukat Khanum hospital") after treatment

On 3 November 2022, Khan was shot in the leg when gunmen opened fire on his convoy in [Wazirabad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wazirabad "Wazirabad") while he was leading a march to [Islamabad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamabad "Islamabad") demanding early elections.[&#8202;](#ref-back-259-0)[\[253\]](#refnote-259) One of his supporters died in the attack.[&#8202;](#ref-back-260-0)[\[254\]](#refnote-260) The alleged perpetrator, Muhammad Naveed, was overpowered by a supporter of Khan, throwing off his aim.[&#8202;](#ref-back-261-0)[\[255\]](#refnote-261) Naveed said he acted alone, targeting him because he believed Khan was misleading the people.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Saeed-0)[\[256\]](#refnote-Saeed)[&#8202;](#ref-back-263-0)[\[257\]](#refnote-263) Khan blamed the incumbent government—Prime Minister [Shehbaz Sharif](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shehbaz_Sharif "Shehbaz Sharif"), Interior Minister [Rana Sanaullah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rana_Sanaullah "Rana Sanaullah"), and Major General [Faisal Naseer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Faisal_Naseer "Faisal Naseer")—for the assassination plot but provided no evidence. Government and military officials denied involvement. Federal Information Minister [Maryam Aurangzeb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maryam_Aurangzeb "Maryam Aurangzeb") questioned how Khan could demand resignations before any investigation. The [Inter-Services Public Relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inter-Services_Public_Relations "Inter-Services Public Relations") called the allegations baseless and irresponsible. Sanaullah also rejected them as grievous.[&#8202;](#ref-back-264-0)[\[258\]](#refnote-264)[&#8202;](#ref-back-265-0)[\[259\]](#refnote-265)

### Arrest and case proceedings

Further information: [Arrest of Imran Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arrest_of_Imran_Khan "Arrest of Imran Khan") and [2023 Pakistani protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Pakistani_protests "2023 Pakistani protests")

Following an arrest warrant, the Islamabad Police and Lahore Police moved to arrest Khan on 14 March 2023.[&#8202;](#ref-back-266-0)[\[260\]](#refnote-266) On 9 May, he was arrested at the Islamabad High Court in connection with the [Al-Qadir Trust case](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Qadir_Trust_case "Al-Qadir Trust case").[&#8202;](#ref-back-267-0)[\[261\]](#refnote-267) His arrest triggered the [May 9 riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/May_9_riots "May 9 riots"), with PTI supporters storming government buildings and military installations.[&#8202;](#ref-back-268-0)[\[262\]](#refnote-268) The Supreme Court of Pakistan declared the arrest illegal, citing the violation of judicial sanctity by paramilitary forces.[&#8202;](#ref-back-269-0)[\[263\]](#refnote-269) On 12 May, he received protective bail, which barred re-arrest on the same charges for two weeks.[&#8202;](#ref-back-270-0)[\[264\]](#refnote-270)

On 5 August, he was arrested again and sentenced to three years after being found guilty of misusing his premiership to buy and sell state gifts, valued over 140 million [rupees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistani_rupees "Pakistani rupees").[&#8202;](#ref-back-271-0)[\[265\]](#refnote-271) On 29 August, an appeals court suspended his corruption conviction and prison term, granting him bail.[&#8202;](#ref-back-272-0)[\[266\]](#refnote-272) A special court ordered that he remain in jail in the [cypher case](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cypher_No._I-0678#Lawsuit "Cypher No. I-0678"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-273-0)[\[267\]](#refnote-273) in which he was accused of leaking state secrets and violating the [Official Secrets Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Official_Secrets_Act_(Pakistan) "Official Secrets Act (Pakistan)").[&#8202;](#ref-back-274-0)[\[268\]](#refnote-274) In 2024, he was convicted and sentenced in the cypher case,[&#8202;](#ref-back-BBC-0)[\[269\]](#refnote-BBC) the Toshakhana-I case,[&#8202;](#ref-back-276-0)[\[270\]](#refnote-276) and the [Iddat case](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iddat_case "Iddat case").[&#8202;](#ref-back-277-0)[\[271\]](#refnote-277) The convictions in the cypher and _iddat_ cases were subsequently overturned,[&#8202;](#ref-back-278-0)[\[272\]](#refnote-278)[&#8202;](#ref-back-279-0)[\[273\]](#refnote-279) while the 14-year sentence in the Toshakhana-I case remained suspended pending appeal as of August 2026.[&#8202;](#ref-back-280-0)[\[274\]](#refnote-280)

In January 2025, he was sentenced to 14 years' imprisonment by an accountability court in the Al-Qadir Trust case.[&#8202;](#ref-back-fp-0)[\[275\]](#refnote-fp) The case alleges that Khan and his wife Bushra obtained billions of rupees and land worth hundreds of [kanals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kanal_(unit) "Kanal (unit)") from [Bahria Town](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bahria_Town "Bahria Town") in connection with the alleged legalisation of Rs50 billion that had been returned to Pakistan by the UK authorities.[&#8202;](#ref-back-282-0)[\[276\]](#refnote-282) Funds recovered by the UK's [National Crime Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Crime_Agency "National Crime Agency") were alleged to be Pakistani state funds and were transferred to Supreme Court accounts to offset Bahria Town's liability from a settlement over illegally acquired land. In the Al-Qadir Trust case, Khan and Bushra, the trust's sole trustees, were alleged to have obtained over 458 acres land from Bahria Town for [Al-Qadir University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Qadir_University "Al-Qadir University"), [Jhelum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jhelum "Jhelum").[&#8202;](#ref-back-283-0)[\[277\]](#refnote-283)

In December 2025, he was sentenced to 17 years' imprisonment and fined Rs16.4 million in the Toshakhana-II case. The court found that a [Bulgari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bulgari "Bulgari") jewellery set gifted by Saudi Crown Prince [Mohammed bin Salman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammed_bin_Salman "Mohammed bin Salman"), valued at about Rs80 million, was acquired by Khan for Rs2.9 million. It credited time served and described the sentence as lenient due to his old age.[&#8202;](#ref-back-284-0)[\[278\]](#refnote-284)

### Imprisonment and jail conditions

Further information: [Imprisonment of Imran Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imprisonment_of_Imran_Khan "Imprisonment of Imran Khan")

Khan says his imprisonment is politically motivated, alleging involvement by the Pakistan Armed Forces (the [Establishment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Establishment_(Pakistan) "Establishment (Pakistan)")) and [Shehbaz Sharif](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shehbaz_Sharif "Shehbaz Sharif")'s government.[&#8202;](#ref-back-285-0)[\[279\]](#refnote-285) Both the military and government denied the allegations; the military called them "fabricated and malicious" while Sharif termed them "blatant lies".[&#8202;](#ref-back-286-0)[\[280\]](#refnote-286) Since his arrest, he has been held in [Adiala Jail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adiala_Jail "Adiala Jail") in [Rawalpindi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rawalpindi "Rawalpindi"), where the special court conducts his trial.[&#8202;](#ref-back-BBC-1)[\[269\]](#refnote-BBC)

In June 2024, the [Working Group on Arbitrary Detention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Working_Group_on_Arbitrary_Detention "Working Group on Arbitrary Detention") called for his release, saying his detention was arbitrary and politically motivated.[&#8202;](#ref-back-287-0)[\[281\]](#refnote-287) In a July interview with _[The Sunday Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Sunday_Times "The Sunday Times")_ from prison, he said he is held in a small " [death cell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_cell "Death cell")" usually "reserved for terrorists".[&#8202;](#ref-back-288-0)[\[282\]](#refnote-288) In August 2024, Khan alleged the [ISI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inter-Services_Intelligence "Inter-Services Intelligence") controls all matters of his imprisonment and has made his jail conditions harsher.[&#8202;](#ref-back-DH-0)[\[283\]](#refnote-DH) In September 2024, _Amnesty International_ said it had "noted a pattern of weaponisation of the legal system to keep him detained and away from political activity" and called for his immediate release.[&#8202;](#ref-back-290-0)[\[284\]](#refnote-290) In October 2024, authorities said he undergoes a medical examination every two weeks and no health issues have been observed.[&#8202;](#ref-back-291-0)[\[285\]](#refnote-291)

On 24 November, Khan's supporters attempted [nationwide protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/November_2024_PTI_protest "November 2024 PTI protest") demanding his release.[&#8202;](#ref-back-292-0)[\[286\]](#refnote-292) Since the 2022 no-confidence vote, he has been named in 186 legal cases across Pakistan, as reported in December 2024.[&#8202;](#ref-back-293-0)[\[287\]](#refnote-293)

In February 2026, fourteen former international cricket captains appealed to Pakistan's government, expressing "profound concern" over reports on Khan's health and prison conditions.[&#8202;](#ref-back-294-0)[\[288\]](#refnote-294) That month, more than 40 international public intellectuals, academics and activists issued a humanitarian appeal calling for Khan's release and urgent access to his family and physicians.[&#8202;](#ref-back-295-0)[\[289\]](#refnote-295)

In August, the Supreme Court ordered Khan's transfer to the private [Shifa International Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shifa_International_Hospitals "Shifa International Hospitals") in Islamabad over blood pressure and anxiety concerns,[&#8202;](#ref-back-296-0)[\[290\]](#refnote-296) but he was instead taken to the state-run [Pakistan Institute of Medical Sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_Institute_of_Medical_Sciences "Pakistan Institute of Medical Sciences") for a medical examination before being returned to his cell.[&#8202;](#ref-back-297-0)[\[291\]](#refnote-297) During the hospital visit, his sister, who is also a doctor and personally checked his blood pressure, said that Khan was "completely healthy and fit", with his blood pressure at 120/80 and his eyesight having improved.[&#8202;](#ref-back-298-0)[\[292\]](#refnote-298)

### 2024 Pakistani general election

In December 2023, he nominated [Gohar Ali Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gohar_Ali_Khan "Gohar Ali Khan") to replace him as PTI chairman, with Gohar being elected unopposed.[&#8202;](#ref-back-299-0)[\[293\]](#refnote-299) Later that month, the ECP rejected his nomination papers for the [2024 Pakistani general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Pakistani_general_election "2024 Pakistani general election") on the grounds that he was "convicted by the court of law and has been disqualified".[&#8202;](#ref-back-300-0)[\[294\]](#refnote-300) Before the elections, his name was barred from the media, and _[TIME](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TIME_(magazine) "TIME (magazine)")_ reported that constituency boundaries were redrawn to allegedly benefit his opponents.[&#8202;](#ref-back-TIME-0)[\[295\]](#refnote-TIME)

A PTI official said he was allowed to vote by postal ballot from prison.[&#8202;](#ref-back-302-0)[\[296\]](#refnote-302) He issued an [AI-generated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AI-generated "AI-generated") message urging supporters to celebrate the electoral win, in which at least 90 candidates backed by him, all of whom were forced to run as independents, won seats.[&#8202;](#ref-back-303-0)[\[297\]](#refnote-303) PTI chairman Gohar Ali Khan said Khan directed PTI-backed independents to form governments in the Centre, [Khyber Pakhtunkhwa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khyber_Pakhtunkhwa "Khyber Pakhtunkhwa"), and [Punjab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punjab,_Pakistan "Punjab, Pakistan"). Gohar said Khan told his party to sit in opposition nationally rather than join a coalition with rival parties, including the [Pakistan Muslim League (N)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_Muslim_League_(N) "Pakistan Muslim League (N)") and [Pakistan Peoples Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_Peoples_Party "Pakistan Peoples Party").[&#8202;](#ref-back-304-0)[\[298\]](#refnote-304)

## Wealth and assets

In his 2003 statement to the Election Commission of Pakistan (ECP), Khan declared his home in [Zaman Park, Lahore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zaman_Park,_Lahore "Zaman Park, Lahore"), an Islamabad apartment, 39 [kanals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kanal_(unit) "Kanal (unit)")[&#8202;](#ref-back-305-0)[\[g\]](#refnote-305) in Islamabad, 530 kanals[&#8202;](#ref-back-306-0)[\[h\]](#refnote-306) in [Khanewal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khanewal "Khanewal"), and a share in 363 kanals[&#8202;](#ref-back-307-0)[\[i\]](#refnote-307) of inherited agricultural land.[&#8202;](#ref-back-308-0)[\[299\]](#refnote-308)

In a 2011 _[Financial Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Financial_Times "Financial Times")_ interview, Khan said he bought a penthouse in [South Kensington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Kensington "South Kensington") in 1983 for £110,000 and sold it in 2003 to buy land in Islamabad.[&#8202;](#ref-back-309-0)[\[300\]](#refnote-309)

In 2017, the ECP reported that Khan's 300-kanal [Bani Gala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bani_Gala "Bani Gala") residence in Islamabad was valued at Rs.750 million (US$2.7million), with other assets including furniture worth Rs.0.6 million (US$2,100) and livestock.[&#8202;](#ref-back-310-0)[\[301\]](#refnote-310) In 2020, the ECP reported that he declared assets worth Rs80.6 million, including a six-kanal plot in [Mohra Noori](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohra_Noori "Mohra Noori") (Rs0.5 million) and five inherited plots in [Mianwali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mianwali "Mianwali"), [Bhakkar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhakkar "Bhakkar"), [Sheikhupura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheikhupura "Sheikhupura"), and Khanewal. He sold property in [Ferozewala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferozewala "Ferozewala") for Rs70 million and bought two [Shahrah-e-Dastoor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shahrah-e-Dastoor "Shahrah-e-Dastoor") apartments for Rs10.19 million. Khan held Rs50.66 million in a bank account, Rs10.99 million in cash, and four [foreign currency accounts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_currency_account "Foreign currency account") with £518, $328,760, $1,470, and an empty euro account. He also declared four goats worth Rs200,000.[&#8202;](#ref-back-311-0)[\[302\]](#refnote-311)

In his 2024 election nomination papers, Khan declared over Rs90 million in bank accounts, more than $300,000 in a foreign currency account, and over a dozen properties, mostly inherited, including agricultural land and his Zaman Park residence. He paid Rs11.97 million for a [Shahrah-e-Dastoor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shahrah-e-Dastoor "Shahrah-e-Dastoor") apartment and declared Rs11.47 million for his Bani Gala residence. He reported owning no vehicle.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Assets2024-0)[\[303\]](#refnote-Assets2024)

### Taxes

_[The News International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_News_International "The News International")_ reported that Khan paid nearly Rs4.7 million in taxes between 1981 and 2017, with exemptions in several years.[&#8202;](#ref-back-313-0)[\[304\]](#refnote-313) In January 2022, the Federal Board of Revenue's 2019 tax directory said that he paid Rs9.8 million in taxes that year.[&#8202;](#ref-back-314-0)[\[305\]](#refnote-314) In 2022, his income rose to Rs185.68 million due to the sale of a watch gifted by a foreign dignitary; the year before, it was just over Rs7 million.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Assets2024-1)[\[303\]](#refnote-Assets2024) For the fiscal year ending 30 June 2023, he paid Rs15.59 million in taxes, and his total declared assets were Rs315.95 million.[&#8202;](#ref-back-315-0)[\[306\]](#refnote-315)

## Political positions

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/30/Imran_Khan_%284276439704%29.jpg/250px-Imran_Khan_%284276439704%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Imran_Khan_(4276439704).jpg) Khan speaking at the [Chatham House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chatham_House "Chatham House") in London, 2012.

In November 1999, Khan condemned the UN for failing to halt Russian "atrocities" in Chechnya.[&#8202;](#ref-back-316-0)[\[307\]](#refnote-316)

After the [2003 invasion of Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2003_invasion_of_Iraq "2003 invasion of Iraq") began, Khan participated in a [protest in Hyde Park, London](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protests_against_the_Iraq_War "Protests against the Iraq War"), saying: "The fear is that this is not going to be the last war; first it was Afghanistan, now it's Iraq, and if the hawks in Washington have their way, then it's going to be some other country very soon."[&#8202;](#ref-back-317-0)[\[308\]](#refnote-317)

A January 2010 [leaked US diplomatic cable](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_diplomatic_cables_leak "United States diplomatic cables leak") released by [WikiLeaks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WikiLeaks "WikiLeaks") revealed that US ambassador to Pakistan [Anne W. Patterson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anne_W._Patterson "Anne W. Patterson") met Khan at his residence, where he criticised the US for its "dangerous" policies, including drone operations.[&#8202;](#ref-back-318-0)[\[309\]](#refnote-318)

In 2011, Khan became the first Pakistani dignitary to demand an official apology from the Government of Pakistan to the people of [Bangladesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladesh "Bangladesh") for the Pakistan Army's atrocities during the [Bangladesh Liberation War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladesh_Liberation_War "Bangladesh Liberation War") in 1971. He said he had initially supported the operation due to Pakistan's lack of independent media but later learned the truth from Bengali friends in England, adding that Pakistan must learn from past mistakes and not repeat them in [Balochistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balochistan,_Pakistan "Balochistan, Pakistan") or the [tribal areas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FATA "FATA").[&#8202;](#ref-back-319-0)[\[310\]](#refnote-319)

[Play video](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Prime_Minister_Imran_Khan's_Message_On_The_Srebrenica_Genocide.webm "Play video")Prime Minister Khan's message on the 25th Memorial Day of the [Srebrenica Genocide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Srebrenica_Genocide "Srebrenica Genocide"), 2020

He has described himself as a [pacifist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacifist "Pacifist") and anti-war and has opposed military interventions, especially in Afghanistan, criticising Pakistan's role in the U.S.-led [war on terror](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_on_terror "War on terror").[&#8202;](#ref-back-320-0)[\[311\]](#refnote-320) He has opposed the [Iraq War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraq_War "Iraq War"), the [Russian Invasion of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Invasion_of_Ukraine "Russian Invasion of Ukraine"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-321-0)[\[312\]](#refnote-321) the [Gaza genocide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaza_genocide "Gaza genocide"), and the [Israeli invasion of Lebanon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Israeli_invasion_of_Lebanon "2024 Israeli invasion of Lebanon").[&#8202;](#ref-back-322-0)[\[313\]](#refnote-322)

In 2013, Khan proposed secret talks between India and Pakistan to resolve the [Kashmir issue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashmir_issue "Kashmir issue"), saying open negotiations risk being subverted by vested interests on both sides.[&#8202;](#ref-back-323-0)[\[314\]](#refnote-323)

In 2014, when the Pakistani Taliban announced armed struggle against [Ismailis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ismailis "Ismailis") and the [Kalash people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalash_people "Kalash people"), Khan described "forced conversions as un-Islamic."[&#8202;](#ref-back-324-0)[\[315\]](#refnote-324) He also condemned the " [forced conversion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coerced_religious_conversion_in_Pakistan "Coerced religious conversion in Pakistan")" of Hindu girls in Sindh.[&#8202;](#ref-back-325-0)[\[316\]](#refnote-325)

## Public image

In the 1980s, Khan sported a " [playboy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Playboy_(lifestyle) "Playboy (lifestyle)")" image in the British press for his exploits on the London party circuit, though he said he never consumed [liquor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liquor "Liquor").[&#8202;](#ref-back-WP_profile-1)[\[91\]](#refnote-WP_profile) In the early 1990s, the British media called him the Sexiest Man Alive.[&#8202;](#ref-back-326-0)[\[317\]](#refnote-326) He was known to millions of cricket fans as the _Lion of Lahore_.[&#8202;](#ref-back-it170217-0)[\[318\]](#refnote-it170217)

In 1996, _[The Wall Street Journal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Wall_Street_Journal "The Wall Street Journal")_ said he used populist rhetoric, blending anti-elite messages with religious appeals. His criticism of Westernised Pakistani elites contrasted with his privileged upbringing and western " [jet-setter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jet-setter "Jet-setter")" lifestyle.[&#8202;](#ref-back-328-0)[\[319\]](#refnote-328) He said of his past: "I have never claimed to be an angel. I am a humble sinner."[&#8202;](#ref-back-2006_profile-1)[\[74\]](#refnote-2006_profile) In a 2006 interview, [Peter Lloyd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_Lloyd_(journalist) "Peter Lloyd (journalist)") described his transformation as a "playboy to puritan U-turn," leaving many scratching their heads in wonder.[&#8202;](#ref-back-foreign_correspondent_feature-0)[\[320\]](#refnote-foreign_correspondent_feature) In 2007, _[Der Spiegel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Der_Spiegel "Der Spiegel")_ compared him to [Franz Beckenbauer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franz_Beckenbauer "Franz Beckenbauer") for his popularity and influence in Pakistan.[&#8202;](#ref-back-330-0)[\[321\]](#refnote-330) In 2018, _[Reuters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reuters "Reuters")_ described him as a Pakistani cricket icon and former London playboy who had "transformed himself into a pious, firebrand [nationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationalist "Nationalist")".[&#8202;](#ref-back-331-0)[\[322\]](#refnote-331)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7f/Imran_Khan_giving_autographs.JPG/250px-Imran_Khan_giving_autographs.JPG?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Imran_Khan_giving_autographs.JPG) Imran Khan signs autographs for supporters, 2009

In June 2011, a [Pew Research Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center "Pew Research Center") poll showed him with a 68% approval rating, higher than Prime Minister [Yousaf Raza Gillani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yousaf_Raza_Gillani "Yousaf Raza Gillani")'s 37% and President [Asif Ali Zardari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asif_Ali_Zardari "Asif Ali Zardari")'s 11%. That year, _[The Washington Post](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Washington_Post "The Washington Post")_ described him as an underdog, saying he "often sounds like a pro-democracy liberal but is well known for his coziness with conservative Islamist parties." [H. M. Naqvi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H._M._Naqvi "H. M. Naqvi") called him a "sort of a [Ron Paul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ron_Paul "Ron Paul") figure," noting "there is no taint of corruption and there is his anti-establishment message."[&#8202;](#ref-back-332-0)[\[323\]](#refnote-332)

His perceived sympathy for the [Taliban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taliban "Taliban") and criticism of the US-led [war on terror](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_on_terror "War on terror") led critics to label him "Taliban Khan." He said, "I've been called Taliban Khan for supporting the tribal Pashtuns and I've been called part of a Jewish conspiracy to take over Pakistan. I am of course neither."[&#8202;](#ref-back-333-0)[\[324\]](#refnote-333)

In 2012, [Pankaj Mishra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pankaj_Mishra "Pankaj Mishra"), writing for _[The New York Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_York_Times "The New York Times")_, characterised him as a "cogent picture out of his—and Pakistan's—clashing identities," adding that "his identification with the suffering masses and his attacks on his affluent, English-speaking peers have long been mocked in the living rooms of Lahore and Karachi as the hypocritical ravings of 'Im the Dim' and 'Taliban Khan'—the two favoured [monikers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moniker "Moniker") for him." Mishra concluded, "like all populist politicians, he appears to offer something to everyone. Yet the great differences between his constituencies—socially liberal, upper-middle-class Pakistanis and the deeply conservative residents of Pakistan's tribal areas—seem irreconcilable."[&#8202;](#ref-back-334-0)[\[325\]](#refnote-334)

After the 2013 election, [Mohammed Hanif](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammed_Hanif "Mohammed Hanif"), writing for _[The Guardian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Guardian "The Guardian")_, said that he had support among the educated middle class, but Pakistan's main problem was that it did not have enough educated urban middle-class citizens.[&#8202;](#ref-back-335-0)[\[326\]](#refnote-335)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/12/Interfaith_Christmas_Dinner_2014_%282%29.jpg/250px-Interfaith_Christmas_Dinner_2014_%282%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Interfaith_Christmas_Dinner_2014_(2).jpg) Khan addressing an Interfaith Christmas Dinner in 2014

An August 2018 [Gallup Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gallup_Pakistan "Gallup Pakistan") survey found 52% of Pakistanis believed his tenure as prime minister would be better than the previous government.[&#8202;](#ref-back-336-0)[\[327\]](#refnote-336)[&#8202;](#ref-back-337-0)[\[328\]](#refnote-337) In a 2019 [International Republican Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Republican_Institute "International Republican Institute") poll, 40% of respondents rated his performance "good" and 17% "very good."[&#8202;](#ref-back-338-0)[\[329\]](#refnote-338) A 2021 [Ipsos Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ipsos "Ipsos") survey found 55% of respondents said [Imran Khan government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imran_Khan_government "Imran Khan government") was worse than they expected.[&#8202;](#ref-back-339-0)[\[330\]](#refnote-339) In a 2022 Gallup Pakistan poll, 48% of respondents had a negative view of his performance, while 36% held a favourable opinion.[&#8202;](#ref-back-340-0)[\[331\]](#refnote-340) Another Gallup Pakistan survey, conducted after his [vote-of-no confidence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/No-confidence_motion_against_Imran_Khan "No-confidence motion against Imran Khan"), found 57% of respondents were happy about his removal from office, while 43% were angry.[&#8202;](#ref-back-341-0)[\[332\]](#refnote-341) A February 2023 Gallup Pakistan survey, conducted after the end of his premiership, found that 61% of Pakistanis held a good opinion of him.[&#8202;](#ref-back-342-0)[\[333\]](#refnote-342)

His arrest and imprisonment in August 2023 angered many in [Indian-administered Kashmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian-administered_Kashmir "Indian-administered Kashmir").[&#8202;](#ref-back-343-0)[\[334\]](#refnote-343)

According to a January 2024 _[Bloomberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bloomberg_News "Bloomberg News")_ survey of 12 Pakistani financial professionals, Khan was the top pick to run the country's economy.[&#8202;](#ref-back-344-0)[\[335\]](#refnote-344)

Khan has been nominated twice for the Nobel Peace Prize. He was nominated in 2019 for his endeavours to foster peace across South Asia. In 2025, he was nominated by the Norwegian political party [Partiet Sentrum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partiet_Sentrum "Partiet Sentrum") and the advocacy organisation Pakistan World Alliance for his contributions to human rights and democratic values.[&#8202;](#ref-back-345-0)[\[336\]](#refnote-345)

In March 2026, American human rights lawyer [Eric Lewis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eric_Lewis_(human_rights_attorney) "Eric Lewis (human rights attorney)") wrote in _[The Independent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Independent "The Independent")_ that Khan had been "wrongly imprisoned for almost three years" and that "the world community should unite to save him."[&#8202;](#ref-back-346-0)[\[337\]](#refnote-346)

### Relationship with the military

According to Christopher Clary, Assistant Professor of Political Science at the State University of New York-Albany, he entered politics in the mid-1990s in open alliance with former [Inter-Services Intelligence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inter-Services_Intelligence "Inter-Services Intelligence") (ISI) chief [Hamid Gul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamid_Gul "Hamid Gul").[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEClary2022-1)[\[106\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEClary2022) In 2012, author [Fatima Bhutto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatima_Bhutto "Fatima Bhutto") criticised him for "incredible coziness not with the military but with dictatorship," accusing him of defending [General Zia-ul-Haq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Zia-ul-Haq "General Zia-ul-Haq")'s legacy and mentioning his support for [Pervez Musharraf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pervez_Musharraf "Pervez Musharraf")'s 2002 referendum.[&#8202;](#ref-back-347-0)[\[338\]](#refnote-347)

According to Clary, Khan was also "close to another former ISI head, [Shuja Pasha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shuja_Pasha "Shuja Pasha")". Clary said that during the [2014 Tsunami March](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Tsunami_March "2014 Tsunami March") and sit-in, there were widespread allegations of involvement by then ISI chief [Zaheer-ul-Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zaheer-ul-Islam "Zaheer-ul-Islam").[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEClary2022-2)[\[106\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEClary2022) Historian Ian Talbot wrote that Khan's role in the 2014 protests was contentious. Talbot stated that Khan "denied that he was a military cat's paw", which Talbot said conflicted with former PTI president [Javed Hashmi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Javed_Hashmi "Javed Hashmi")'s claim that the protests were inspired by Pasha. Talbot said that his actions, "if not sinister, were reckless", threatening hopes of rebalancing civil–military relations in Pakistan.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTETalbot2016-0)[\[339\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTETalbot2016)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTETalbot2022-0)[\[340\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTETalbot2022) He stated that these actions strengthened military authority over civilian power,[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTETalbot2016-1)[\[339\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTETalbot2016) and that "the army rather than the democratic forces had clearly emerged as a winner in the political crisis that some believed it had secretly orchestrated".[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTETalbot2022-1)[\[340\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTETalbot2022)

According to Mohammad Waseem, Professor of Political Science at the Lahore University of Management Sciences, his populist rise was enabled by the military establishment, which sought to counter the [PPP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_People's_Party "Pakistan People's Party") and [PML (N)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PML_(N) "PML (N)"). Waseem said he was cultivated by the establishment as an alternative force, with the [PTI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_Tehreek-e-Insaf "Pakistan Tehreek-e-Insaf") functioning as a "shadow" Muslim League to attract electables before the [2018 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Pakistani_general_election "2018 Pakistani general election"). Waseem said that he appealed to middle-class demands to end dynastic politics and came to power not through mass mobilisation, but military support. He added that he was portrayed at home and abroad as the military's preferred candidate, fuelling opposition claims that he was "selected" rather than elected.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEWaseem202217,_155,_176,_180,_181,_208,_275,_293-0)[\[341\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEWaseem202217,_155,_176,_180,_181,_208,_275,_293)

US diplomat Theodore Craig, in _Pakistan and American Diplomacy_, wrote that after the 2018 elections the United States avoided pushing for an "unblemished election" or rejecting "antidemocratic manipulations", saying that challenging "the military's [Imran Khan project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Project_Imran_Khan "Project Imran Khan")" would not have changed the government but could have jeopardised prospects for progress in Afghanistan.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTECraig202413–14;_17-0)[\[342\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTECraig202413–14;_17)

In 2024, _The New York Times_ wrote that Lt. Gen. [Faiz Hameed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Faiz_Hameed "Faiz Hameed"), a former ISI chief, was an ally of his.[&#8202;](#ref-back-352-0)[\[343\]](#refnote-352) In 2024, _Arab News_ wrote that Khan was widely believed to have risen to power in 2018 with military backing.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ArabNewsFaiz2024-0)[\[344\]](#refnote-ArabNewsFaiz2024) _Arab News_ said that after his 2022 ouster, Hameed was widely believed to have advised him as PTI openly criticised the army and its senior leadership. Since then, Khan has campaigned against the military, blaming it for failing to stop the no-confidence motion that removed him.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ArabNewsFaiz2024-1)[\[344\]](#refnote-ArabNewsFaiz2024)

[Ashok Swain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashok_Swain "Ashok Swain"), Professor of Peace and Conflict Research at Uppsala University, Sweden, wrote that his challenges and accusations against the military are unique in Pakistan's history. Swain said he became the military's strongest critic, with potential to reform its historically unchecked power. His approach has divided allies and the military but, according to Swain, "mobilised a new generation of politically aware Pakistanis, including women and youth who previously shunned politics".[&#8202;](#ref-back-scroll041224-0)[\[345\]](#refnote-scroll041224)

In a 2025 book, author Dinkar Prakash Srivastava wrote that Khan enjoyed military patronage and "colluded" with it against Prime Minister [Nawaz Sharif](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nawaz_Sharif "Nawaz Sharif"). He wrote that the army "paved the way" for his 2018 election victory by easing out Sharif's government before the end of its term. Khan's relations with the military began to deteriorate in 2021 over the appointment of the ISI chief. After his ouster in 2022, Khan blamed army chief General [Qamar Javed Bajwa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qamar_Javed_Bajwa "Qamar Javed Bajwa") and criticised the military leadership, including General [Asim Munir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asim_Munir "Asim Munir") and the [ISPR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inter-Services_Public_Relations "Inter-Services Public Relations"). Srivastava wrote that if Khan sought to divide the top military leadership, it "backfired spectacularly" as the army "does not tolerate interference" in its internal affairs, and that Khan "found himself accidentally thrust into the role of an anti-establishment hero", despite owing his political career to military patronage.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTESrivastava2025-0)[\[346\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTESrivastava2025)

In December 2025, [Mohammed Hanif](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammed_Hanif "Mohammed Hanif") wrote that early in his political career, Khan found out that "the road to power in Pakistan always passed through the army headquarters," and they helped him rise by sidelining his opponents. After falling out with them, his name and image were suppressed on television, and his access to family and lawyers was restricted.[&#8202;](#ref-back-356-0)[\[347\]](#refnote-356)

### In popular culture

See also: [Category:Cultural depictions of Imran Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Category:Cultural_depictions_of_Imran_Khan "Category:Cultural depictions of Imran Khan")

During his cricketing days, he appeared in commercials for Pepsi Pakistan, [Brooke Bond](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brooke_Bond "Brooke Bond") tea, [Thums Up](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thums_Up "Thums Up"), and the Indian soap Cinthol.[&#8202;](#ref-back-357-0)[\[348\]](#refnote-357) [Dev Anand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dev_Anand "Dev Anand") offered him a role in his 1990 [Bollywood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bollywood "Bollywood") sports action-thriller _[Awwal Number](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Awwal_Number "Awwal Number")_ as a declining cricket star, but he refused, citing his lack of acting skills.[&#8202;](#ref-back-358-0)[\[349\]](#refnote-358) In 2014, Canadian rock band [Nickelback](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nickelback "Nickelback") released the politically themed single "Edge of a Revolution", featuring a brief clip of a PTI rally with party flags and a poster of him.[&#8202;](#ref-back-359-0)[\[350\]](#refnote-359)

Chacha Nooruddin, known as _Captain Chappal_,[&#8202;](#ref-back-360-0)[\[351\]](#refnote-360) gifted a pair of specially crafted [Peshawari chappals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peshawari_chappal "Peshawari chappal") to him for his 2015 wedding. The double-soled design had existed for years,[&#8202;](#ref-back-dawn2015-0)[\[352\]](#refnote-dawn2015) but the traditional Peshawari chappal became iconic as his preferred footwear.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ArabNews-0)[\[353\]](#refnote-ArabNews)

In the 2021 Indian film [_83_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/83_(film) "83 (film)"), about the [1983 Cricket World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1983_Cricket_World_Cup "1983 Cricket World Cup"), he is portrayed by actor [Chunky Panday](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chunky_Panday "Chunky Panday").[&#8202;](#ref-back-363-0)[\[354\]](#refnote-363)

## Awards and honours

Main article: [List of awards and honours received by Imran Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_awards_and_honours_received_by_Imran_Khan "List of awards and honours received by Imran Khan")

## Authorship

In the late 1980s, Khan served as editor of _[The Cricketer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Cricketer "The Cricketer")_, a London-based cricket magazine.[&#8202;](#ref-back-364-0)[\[355\]](#refnote-364) Khan has written opinion pieces for _[Outlook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Outlook_(Indian_magazine) "Outlook (Indian magazine)")_,[&#8202;](#ref-back-365-0)[\[356\]](#refnote-365) _[The Guardian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Guardian "The Guardian")_,[&#8202;](#ref-back-366-0)[\[357\]](#refnote-366) and the _[BBC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BBC "BBC")_.[&#8202;](#ref-back-367-0)[\[358\]](#refnote-367) Khan's 2011 autobiography, _[Pakistan: A Personal History](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan:_A_Personal_History "Pakistan: A Personal History")_, covers his move from cricket into politics and discusses his philanthropic work.[&#8202;](#ref-back-368-0)[\[359\]](#refnote-368) In 2021, Khan penned a _[CNN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CNN "CNN")_ op-ed advocating conservation and restoration of damaged natural ecosystems.[&#8202;](#ref-back-369-0)[\[360\]](#refnote-369)

Khan's publications include:

- _West and East_ (Macmillan, 1975)[&#8202;](#ref-back-370-0)[\[361\]](#refnote-370)
- _Imran: The Autobiography of Imran Khan_ (Pelham, 1983)
- _Imran Khan's Cricket Skills_ (Hamlyn, 1989)
- _Indus Journey: A Personal View of Pakistan_ (Chatto & Windus, 1991)
- _[All Round View](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All_Round_View "All Round View")_ (Mandarin, 1992)
- _Warrior Race: A Journey Through the Land of the Tribal Pathans_ (Chatto & Windus, 1993)
- _[Pakistan: A Personal History](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan:_A_Personal_History "Pakistan: A Personal History")_ (Bantam Press, 2011)[&#8202;](#ref-back-371-0)[\[362\]](#refnote-371)
- _Main Aur Mera Pakistan_ (Orient, 2014)[&#8202;](#ref-back-372-0)[\[363\]](#refnote-372)

[↑](#ref-back-Morgan-0)[Morgan (2012)](#CITEREFMorgan2012) "We'd heard from our usual inside source that the Sunday Mirror were breaking a story about Imran Khan fathering a lovechild by Sita White, daughter of the business tycoon Lord White. The picture desk sent a photographer straight to her home in Los Angeles, and when she opened the door he was able to take one quick shot inside. On closer examination of his picture he spotted a framed photo on the table in her hallway – with Sita and the lovechild who bears a striking resemblance to his father."

The treatment was very expensive, and needed doing in London, but General Zia guaranteed that the government would supply the money. So in the spring of 1984, Imran travelled to the Cromwell Hospital in London for a six-month course of treatment, during which his leg was put in a cast. Neither he, nor anyone else, knew whether he would play cricket again...."I had the choice of continuing as captain following that series, but decided against it.
